- **No pair's residual has its low point more than one frame from offset 0.** Inside the crop, `cam0`–`cam1` and `cam2`–`cam5` are lowest at 0, four pairs with camera B read one frame later, and `cam6`–`cam8` with it read one frame earlier.
- **Where an offset helps, it helps a little.** The largest gain is `cam6`–`cam7`'s, from 2.70 to 2.26 px at +1, a sixth of its median; the other four gain 4% to 10%. Frame by frame, the offset that helps lowers the median in 59% to 64% of a pair's frames, by 0.06 to 0.15 px at the median over the frames.
- **Outside the crop** the median does not move with the offset in four pairs (within 0.07 px) and little in `cam1`–`cam4` (within 0.4 px, around 4.6 px). In `cam6`–`cam7` and `cam6`–`cam8` it moves the way it does inside, on the 1% of those pairs' matches that have neither end in a rectangle: they behave as the person does, not as a background.
- **Nothing else improves.** Coverage inside the crop is highest at offset 0 in every pair. D-22's filters keep 62.4% of the drawn matches inside the crop at offset 0, 60.0% and 56.6% at +1 and −1, and 54.0% and 50.5% at +2 and −2.
- **How much grip the test has.** The control of one camera at two consecutive frames (the nine cameras, every tenth frame) gives the person's motion: inside the crop the admitted matches move 0.28 px per frame at the median and 4.2 px at the 90th percentile, where `kitchen`'s dynamic objects move 8.9 px. So an offset can move the median only a little, and it does move it, upward: over the seven pairs from 1.84 px to 2.00 and 2.16 px one frame off, and to 2.32 and 2.58 px two frames off.

So the cameras are in step to within a frame. A lag of a fraction of a frame between the two cameras is not excluded in five of the pairs, but it would account for a sixth of one pair's residual at most and a tenth or less of the others'. Timing is not what the disagreement is made of.

**Placeholder pixels: the rule.** On a pixel's 8-bit values, read from the scene's 1,080 images (row 614), m = min(R, B) − G, how far the pixel leans to magenta. A pixel is a placeholder pixel when m ≥ 64. Each pixel is judged alone: no smoothing, no neighbourhood and no other frame enters. Two more rules bracket it: tight, m ≥ 128; loose, m ≥ 64, or m ≥ 32 with R − B < 32.

Why 64: the nine cameras show one scene, so a colour that only one camera has is not the scene's. Over all 120 frames, 2,630 of every million pixels of `cam0` have m ≥ 64, and 0.2 of every million pixels of the other eight cameras together (192 pixels in 904 million, never more than 12 in a frame). On `cam0` those pixels are magenta: their median colour is (R, G, B) = (206, 38, 219), half of them have R between 184 and 219, G between 25 and 54 and B between 199 and 230, and R is within 32 of B for 79% of them. Below 64 every camera holds a second, pink population, 1.6% to 2.1% of its pixels, around (R, G, B) = (240, 112, 158), with R above B by about 56 to 95; it is as large on each of the nine cameras, so it is taken as the scene's own colour and is not counted. On `cam0` alone a ramp of the magenta hue runs on below 64, at 75 to 114 pixels per million for every 8 levels of m down to m = 32, where the other cameras together have 20 per million at m 32 to 39 and under 2 from 48 up. These are probably the blended edges of the magenta regions, and the rule at 64 leaves them out. The loose rule takes them in: it adds 365 pixels per million on `cam0`, 14% more, and finds 20 to 52 per million on the other cameras, where the rule at 64 finds under 1.

| `cam1` | 12 | 80 | 0.7 per million | 0; 52 per million | 0, 0, 12 pixels | 1.0 per million | 100% |
| `cam2` | 7 | 36 | 0.3 per million | 0; 20 per million | 0, 0, 8 pixels | 0.5 per million | 100% |
| `cam3` | 4 | 28 | 0.2 per million | 0; 25 per million | 0, 0, 12 pixels | 0.4 per million | 100% |
| `cam4` | 1 | 4 | under 0.1 per million | 0; 40 per million | 0, 0, 4 pixels | 0.1 per million | 100% |
| `cam5` | 1 | 4 | under 0.1 per million | 0; 30 per million | 0, 0, 4 pixels | 0.1 per million | 100% |
| `cam6` | 1 | 4 | under 0.1 per million | 0; 23 per million | 0, 0, 4 pixels | 0.1 per million | 100% |

So the placeholder pixels are `cam0`'s: one pixel in 380 of the camera, one in 225 of its crop, in every frame. No other camera has more than 12 in a frame.

**Placeholder pixels: where they lie.** On `cam0` every one of the 297,359 lies inside the crop, and none within 102 px of its left, right or top side: they span x 198 to 501 and y 430 to 857, the crop's middle columns, between 17% and 59% of its height from the top. They move: 26,478 different pixels are a placeholder pixel in at least one frame (4.75% of the crop), and 280 in at least half of the frames. They are densest in the row of 32 px cells at y 512 to 544, x 320 to 480, where 15% to 26% of a cell's pixels, counted over all frames, are placeholder pixels. Over time the share dips to 0.06% at frame 91 and jumps to 0.449% at frame 92; its mean is 0.238% over frames 0 to 79 and 0.313% over frames 80 to 119, the notes' last third, where the person is closest (row 608).

The matcher admits nothing there. Over the 120 frames of `cam0`–`cam1`, 233,628 of `cam0`'s grid points fall on placeholder pixels, and 19 of them have an admitted match. So placeholder pixels enter neither the residuals above nor the points `roma_init` would make. They reach training only as pixels of `cam0`'s images, where no term of the loss can leave a pixel out today (section 2.3).

**Placeholder pixels: the sheet,** for the owner, in `<surface-runs>/ticket-4/private-scene-A/`. Green paint is a placeholder pixel, yellow a pixel only the loose rule adds, and the white box is rule a's crop on that camera.

- `placeholder_sheet_overview.jpg`: the nine cameras, one per column, at frames 0, 30, 60, 90 and 119, the frames of the notes' own sheets (row 608), and in the last row each camera at its frame with the most placeholder pixels (`cam0`: frame 93). Half size, where a painted pixel stands for any painted pixel of its 2 × 2 block.
- `placeholder_sheet_centre.png`: `cam0` at frames 0, 30, 60, 90, 119 and 93, full size, the image beside the painted image.
- `placeholder_sheet_zoom.png`: the same six frames, the region x 144 to 544, y 384 to 1136, which holds every pixel the loose rule finds in any frame with 32 px to spare, enlarged twice without interpolation, the image beside the painted image.
- `placeholder_sheet_pink.jpg`: the nine cameras at frame 60, the image above and, below it, the pink population painted blue, so the owner can say whether it is the scene's own colour.

On the six full-size panels the green pixels number exactly the placeholder pixels of the frame (2,352, 1,400, 3,336, 1,076, 2,518 and 4,440). The implementer has not seen the sheets or any image: the detections are judged by the numbers above, and **the owner's check of the sheet is pending**. What the owner is asked: whether the green regions are the magenta artifacts, and all of them; whether the yellow pixels belong to them; whether the blue population is the scene's own colour; and whether the white box holds the person on every camera.

**What the figures mean for H2's premise.** H2's premise is that the cameras' images disagree about the person. The figures bear it out, show a second disagreement beside it, and bound both from below only.

- **Against the floor.** On `kitchen` the admitted matches lie 0.02 to 0.04 px from epipolar agreement at the median and 0.08 to 0.12 px at the 90th percentile, at D-22's angles and at 15° alike. On private scene A the matches inside the person's crop lie 1.84 px and 6.61 px from it: 56 and 66 times `kitchen`'s dynamic objects at 15°, and 54 times at the median when both are counted in matcher cells. Per pair the median is 21 to 99 times that floor.
- **Against the scene's own background.** Not all of that is about the person. The background of private scene A reads 0.29 to 2.24 px, steady from frame to frame, and a turn of one camera of the pair by 0.1° to 0.4° brings it to 0.3 to 0.6 px: the manifest's cameras are not quite the cameras the generated images show. That is a disagreement of its own kind, which concerns the whole image and which no change to the person's surface could absorb. With it taken out, the person's matches still lie 0.9 to 4.0 px from agreement at the median and 2.6 to 12.4 px at the 90th percentile. Whichever geometry is taken, the manifest's cameras, the turn fitted to the background or a turn fitted to person and background together, the person's median is some 2 to 13 times the background's. They do so unevenly: the residual swings up to eightfold between frames and is two to four times higher in five pairs once the person is closest.
- **How large that is in the scene.** Reading the manifest's unit as a metre, as the notes do, 1 px of residual at the person's depth of 1.1 is 2 mm across the epipolar line. With the manifest's cameras the person's median is then 2 to 10 mm per pair and the 90th percentile 5 to 27 mm; after the turn, 2 to 8 mm and 5 to 24 mm. The shell sits 5 to 25 cm from the body (section 2.1). So where the matcher can match the person, the two images disagree by about a tenth of the shell's distance, or less.
- **What the residual cannot see.** It is a lower bound twice over. It reads only the part of a disagreement across the epipolar line, one image direction per pair. And it reads only the matches the matcher is certain of: 18% of the crop's grid points, three in ten of them on background seen past the person, where two consecutive frames of one camera, which do agree, give 48.5%. So about three fifths of what can be matched in the crop when two images agree cannot be matched between two cameras. `kitchen`'s static pixels lose a quarter at the same angle (64.6% against 87.4%); its dynamic objects are no control here, since they move 8.9 px in a frame. Whether the cameras disagree more where the matcher gives up than where it matches, the probe cannot say; a disagreement the size of the shell's distance, if there is one, would be there.
- **Initialization already sees it.** D-22's filters turn away 38% of the matches drawn inside the crop, and none on `kitchen`: the init points come from the part of the person the two cameras agree on within 2 px.
- **Timing is not the cause.** No frame offset lowers a pair's residual by more than a sixth, and the cameras are in step to within a frame.
- **Placeholder pixels are a separate defect:** one camera, one pixel in 225 of its crop, in every frame, all on the person, and none of them matched. They are pixels known to be wrong, the case family E's survey looks for (section 3.6).

So the premise holds in kind: the cameras disagree about the person, well beyond the measure's floor and beyond the scene's own background, and more as the person comes closer. In size, what the probe can measure is millimetres to a few centimetres, short of the shell's 5 to 25 cm, on the fifth of the crop that can be matched at all. The probe does not test H2 itself, that the shell is how training reconciles the disagreement. Two things follow for the other sections. A depth estimated per camera and lifted with the manifest's cameras (section 2.7) inherits the tenths of a degree by which those cameras are off, before any disagreement about the person. And a technique that corrects each camera's pose (section 3.6) could remove the background's part of the residual, and not the person's.

**Runs.** `python` is the shared `.venv`'s, run with `PYTHONPATH=<worktree>/src:<worktree>/harness/src:<worktree>/scripts:<scratch>/ticket-4/env-init` and `OMP_NUM_THREADS`, `MKL_NUM_THREADS` and `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS` at the thread count given (the three chained GPU runs of 2026-11-14 were started before the third was required, and held 2.4 cores as measured). Scripts are in `<scratch>/ticket-4`, `<out>` is `<surface-runs>/ticket-4`, and `<gt>` is `$OPENFTGS_DATASETS/synth/ground_truth/kitchen`. An evaluation is one pair, frame and offset.

The GPU ran for 73 minutes in all. The matcher is the vendored RoMa at 77f8d68 with `use_custom_corr` off (fused-local-corr is not installed), as `roma_provenance` records it in each run's `meta.json`. No output file holds private scene A's location or name: the scene is named by `--label`.

### 2.6 Probe: the shell's measures

*Ticket #5's section.* On private scene A's checkpoints, the notes' three measures (the share of opacity farther than 5 cm from every init point, the share of primitives whose duration is under two frames, the distribution of base opacity) and the fixed sheet of novel views from the side, with its render request; on openftgs-synth's M17 checkpoints, the geometry residual and the error of expected depth from `scripts/gt_depth.py` (Q-226 to Q-231).

These are the figures a later training test is compared with (section 1, "No camera is ground truth"). All were measured on 2026-11-14 with the integration branch's code at 841f1bc, whose `src/` and `scripts/` are `main`'s at 93eb58e: Python 3.14, torch 2.15.0+cu132, gsplat 1.5.3, SciPy 1.18.1, NumPy 2.5.4, four CPU threads, one RTX 4090. "The commands", at the section's end, lists every run. No image of private scene A was opened, and no render or sheet of it was looked at: scripts read tensors and JSON, and wrote numbers and files. `<notes>` below is the folder of the owner's notes on private scene A, which holds their runs; like `<private-scene-A>`, it is written nowhere.

**The notes' three measures** (rows 513, 514). The notes' scorer reads two files per run: the exchange model `ftgs export` writes from the checkpoint (§2.2 `ftgs-if`: `position` is μx, `opacity` is E3's base opacity σ, `duration` is E4's duration s in seconds), and the init points file the run trained from.

- **The far-5 cm share.** A primitive is far when its position is more than 0.05 world units, which the notes read as 5 cm, from every init point of that file, all init frames taken together. The measure is the share of the summed base opacity that the far primitives hold. The scorer also keeps the share of primitives that are far.
- **The under-two-frames share.** The share of primitives whose duration is under 2/24 s, two frame intervals at the scene's 24 fps. The duration is the standard deviation of E4's Gaussian in time, not a length of life: a primitive at that bound still has σt ≥ 0.61 over four frame intervals.
- **The distribution of base opacity.** Its mean, and the shares of primitives under 0.05 and over 0.5. This section adds the quartiles and the share under 0.005, D-14's dead threshold.

Every checkpoint of the scene has the spatial scale S = 0.416 (D-27), and the nine cameras are 1.05 from the point where their optical axes meet (rows 515, 517). So 5 cm is 0.12·S, six times the δ = 0.02·S that the geometry residual uses on openftgs-synth (Q-229).

A scratch script computes the three as the scorer does, without the exported file: `load_checkpoint`, the model on the CPU, `FreeTimeGaussians.to_exchange()`, `read_init_points`, an exact nearest-neighbour search (SciPy's `cKDTree`, k = 1), then the scorer's expressions, written anew (row 517).

**The checkpoints the owner still has: 45**, all from 30,000-iteration runs of openftgs 0.1.0 (row 513).

- The final checkpoint of each of the notes' 37 scored runs: 30 keep the centre camera as the test view, and 7 the camera left of it. The notes' three failed or stopped runs left none.
- Five periodic checkpoints, at iterations 5,000 to 25,000, of one of those runs.
- The three models the notes trained on all nine cameras.

A fourth nine-camera model, the default configuration from a stride-1 init, which the notes cite for the artifacts between cameras, is not among them. It is a link from `<notes>` to a place this ticket was not given, so it was not opened ("What is open", below).

**Against the notes' own figures.** The 42 checkpoints of the runs that keep one camera as the test view can be checked, and all 42 agree. The eight figures the scorer writes per checkpoint (N, the two far-5 cm shares, the under-two-frames share, the median duration and the three opacity figures) equal the notes' score files in every digit, though those were written with torch 2.14.1 and these with torch 2.15.0. The 37 rows of the notes' table of all runs are the same figures, rounded. The three nine-camera models have no figure in the notes, so they cannot be checked that way; they are measured by the same code, against the init points file the note beside them names.

**The measures, per checkpoint.** A run is named as the notes name it. "Init points" says how `ftgs init roma` was run (D-21's stride; 1,000 points per init frame, 120,000 in all, unless the cell says otherwise) and how the notes then edited the file, which keeps its positions. The two far-5 cm shares are taken against that file. "Differs" lists what the run's training configuration changes from the default. σ is the base opacity, and its quartiles are the 25th, 50th and 75th percentiles.

**What the measures contain.** This ticket's reading, on seven of the checkpoints (`<surface-runs>/ticket-5/shell_supplement.json`); the notes make none of these splits. The init points fall into two groups by depth along the centre camera's axis: in each of the five files checked, 52 to 58 % lie nearer than 2.0, about 1 % between 1.5 and 2.3, and the rest farther, to 14. The person is taken to be in the nearer group, at 0.7 to 1.5: that is where the init points that project inside the notes' crop of the centre camera lie, and no image was seen to confirm it. "The near side" below is the primitives whose position is nearer than 2.0, and "the far side", or the background, the others. The four init points files named in the header are those of the runs with the centre camera as the test view, and the nine-camera models' file.

| Checkpoint | Far-5 cm share against its own init | Against the stride-1 file | Against the stride-4 file | Against the nine-camera stride-4 file | Against the file with 3,000 points per frame | Share of the opacity on the near side | Far-5 cm share within the near side | Far-5 cm share within the far side | The far-5 cm share's parts: near side + far side |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `A_default_s0` | 0.259 | 0.259 | 0.269 | 0.264 | 0.231 | 0.749 | 0.128 | 0.649 | 0.096 + 0.163 |
| `A_default_s1` | 0.257 | 0.257 | 0.265 | 0.256 | 0.228 | 0.755 | 0.131 | 0.647 | 0.099 + 0.159 |
| `n1500k` | 0.235 | 0.235 | 0.243 | 0.236 | 0.209 | 0.763 | 0.097 | 0.677 | 0.074 + 0.161 |
| `c3_s4` | 0.213 | 0.200 | 0.213 | 0.206 | 0.176 | 0.785 | 0.124 | 0.538 | 0.097 + 0.116 |
| `b15_init_s4` | 0.203 | 0.185 | 0.203 | 0.194 | 0.160 | 0.784 | 0.105 | 0.557 | 0.082 + 0.120 |
| `c3_s4_reg0.1` | 0.161 | 0.150 | 0.161 | 0.155 | 0.129 | 0.861 | 0.083 | 0.647 | 0.071 + 0.090 |
| `stride4` | 0.209 | 0.200 | 0.220 | 0.209 | 0.174 | 0.784 | 0.122 | 0.528 | 0.095 + 0.114 |

- **The far-5 cm share depends on the init points it is taken against.** Against another 120,000-point file of the scene it moves by up to 0.02, and against the file with three times the points it is 0.03 to 0.04 lower: it is a share against a sample of the surface, and it falls as the sample grows. The gain the notes found for a stride-4 init holds against any one file: from `n1500k` to `b15_init_s4` the share goes from 0.235 to 0.203 against each run's own init, from 0.235 to 0.185 against the stride-1 file, and from 0.243 to 0.203 against the stride-4 file.
- **More than half of it is the background.** The far side holds 14 to 25 % of the opacity, and 53 to 68 % of that opacity is far from every init point, against 8 to 13 % on the near side. So 54 to 68 % of the far-5 cm share comes from the far side, where the init points are thin, and the rest from the side the person is on. The near side's own share is 0.128 for the default configuration (0.131 with seed 1), 0.124 with the stride-4 init (`c3_s4`) and 0.122 for the nine-camera model `stride4`.
- **Pooling the init frames was checked once, without a clear answer.** The person comes about 0.5 nearer over the clip, so the init points of all frames together fill the volume the surface sweeps, and a primitive off the surface at its own time can lie within 5 cm of a point from another time. Comparing each primitive only with the init points within 6 frames of its time centre raises the near side's share of the default checkpoint from 0.128 to 0.189. Against the file with three times the points the same window gives 0.161, so much of the rise is the thinner sample, and the check does not separate the two (`<surface-runs>/ticket-5/shell_windowed.json`).

| `c3_s4_reg0.1` | 0.087 | 6.2 / 8.9 / 21.4 | 0.84 | 4.82 (1.39 to 30.18) | 2.08 (1.33 to 3.31) | 0.335 | 0.478 | 0.047 | 0.049 | 0.006 |
| `stride4` | 0.130 | 6.7 / 10.8 / 27.2 | 0.75 | 4.21 (1.03 to 16.17) | 1.61 (1.00 to 2.59) | 0.379 | 0.622 | 0.104 | 0.148 | 0.048 |

- **The far primitives are not the short-lived ones.** On the near side of the default checkpoint, the primitives far from every init point have a median duration of 3.9 frames, and 33 % of them are under two frames; those within 5 cm of an init point have 1.4 frames and 70 %. Their base opacities differ less (medians 0.12 and 0.18). So the two shares count different primitives. The far-5 cm share counts longer-lived primitives away from the triangulated points, more than half of them by opacity in the background. The under-two-frames share counts short-lived primitives close to the triangulated points, on the near side: 65 % of the near side's primitives are under two frames, against 16 % of the far side's. Across the 30 runs with the centre camera as the test view, the rank correlation of the two shares is 0.44.
- **The notes' two medians are the whole model's.** The notes give the shell a median opacity of 0.16 and a median lifetime of two frames (section 2.1). Those are the medians over all 3,000,000 primitives of the default checkpoints: 0.160 and 1.92 frames, and 0.159 and 1.91 with seed 1. Over the primitives far from every init point they are 0.135 and 6.2 frames. The script behind the notes' sentence is not among the kept tools (row 514), so the population it took cannot be checked. The far primitives' distances to the nearest init point have quartiles of 8.5, 18 and 37 cm, wider than the notes' 5 to 25 cm.
- **Between seeds.** Four pairs of runs differ only by seed. The far-5 cm share differs by 0.001 to 0.002 in three pairs and by 0.010 in the fourth, the under-two-frames share by 0.002 to 0.004, and the mean base opacity by 0.001 to 0.003.
- **Against the notes' test-view scores.** Across the 30 runs with the centre camera as the test view, a lower far-5 cm share goes with a higher PSNR on that camera's crop (Spearman's ρ = −0.72) and a lower ꟻLIP (ρ = 0.65), and so does a lower under-two-frames share (−0.60 and 0.69). Neither goes with the notes' sharpness ratio (0.07 and 0.13). Each far-5 cm share is against its run's own init, and the runs differ by configuration, not by seed: the measures moved with the scores under the notes' changes, which is less than saying that lowering them raises a score.
- **Over training.** In the run with periodic checkpoints (`loss.lambda_reg` 0.1), the far-5 cm share falls from 0.275 at iteration 5,000 to 0.164 at 30,000. The under-two-frames share rises from 0.200 to 0.340 at iteration 10,000, and stays near 0.38 from 15,000 on.
- **Dead primitives at the end.** At iteration 30,000, 7 % of the default checkpoints' primitives are under D-14's dead threshold of 0.005; 9 to 10 % for the stride-4 runs at the default cap, and 15 to 17 % for those that also set `loss.lambda_reg` 0.1. At the periodic checkpoints up to iteration 25,000 the share is at most 0.02 %, and that run's final checkpoint has 15 %. Relocation stops at iteration 25,000 (`reloc.stop`), and the primitives that fall under the threshold after it stay where they are.

**The sheet** (row 517). One render request, `<surface-runs>/ticket-5/side_request.json` (39 items; SHA-256 `1b88fd859d64028c9ff40a4624a6bce76edb0be95b8f4129ccfbdc5933d98821`), fixes the cameras and the times. It holds cameras and timestamps and no path, and since the scene manifest and its two copies with a test view share cameras and timestamps, it serves every checkpoint of the scene: `ftgs render CHECKPOINT --request <surface-runs>/ticket-5/side_request.json --out RENDERS.safetensors`.

- **The times:** frames 0, 60 and 119 of the 120 (0 s, 2.5 s and 4.958 s), the three the notes' novel views use.
- **The side poses,** by a rule that reads the scene manifest alone (row 515). P is the point nearest to the nine optical axes, which pass it within 2·10⁻⁷; seen from P, the four cameras beside, above and below the centre camera are 15° from it and the four corner cameras 21.1°. The centre camera is turned rigidly about the axis through P that is parallel to its image's vertical axis, by a yaw of −90°, −60°, −45°, −30°, 0°, 30°, 45°, 60° and 90°, negative toward the left. It keeps its distance to P, its K and its image size, and looks at P. Yaw 0° is the centre camera itself, and yaw ∓15° gives the cameras left and right of it to 10⁻⁷, so 30° is twice as far round as they are. The ids are `sL90`, `sL60`, `sL45`, `sL30`, `sC00`, `sR30`, `sR45`, `sR60` and `sR90`, followed by the frame.
- **The notes' four poses between cameras,** kept so that the sheet joins on to the notes' own pictures: halfway from the centre camera to the camera left of it, to the one above it and to a corner one, and 40 % beyond the camera right of it, with the centre interpolated linearly and the rotation along the shortest arc. Their items are copied from the notes' request (ids `nm01`, `nm03`, `nm05`, `nx02`), after a check against that rule to 10⁻⁹.
- **The rest of the request:** a black background and the model's full SH degree, as in the notes' requests.
- **The sheet's layout:** one JPEG per checkpoint. The 13 poses are the columns, in the request's order, and the three frames the rows. A tile is the render clamped to [0, 1] and scaled by 0.4, with its id; a strip above the tiles carries the checkpoint's name.

Seven checkpoints have a sheet, `<surface-runs>/ticket-5/sheets/side_<name>.jpg`:

- the three nine-camera models, `stride4`, `stride4_zero_velocity` and `stride4_zero_velocity_reg0.1` (files `side_nine-<name>.jpg`), where every pose but `sC00` is a novel view;
- `A_default_s0` and `A_default_s1`, the default configuration at two seeds, which show how much two seeds differ to the eye;
- `c3_s4`, the notes' recommended configuration with the centre camera as the test view, where `sC00` is the test view;
- `h1_init_s4`, the same init with the camera left of the centre as the test view, at a cap of 1,500,000.

Any other kept checkpoint is one `ftgs render` away. What could be checked without looking:

- The request parses with `read_render_request`.
- The model `stride4`'s near-side primitives, weighted by σ_eff at the item's time (E3, E4) and moved by E1, were projected into each item's camera. Their median lands between 0.23 and 0.75 of the frame's width and between 0.58 and 0.65 of its height in every side pose, and 61 to 89 % of their weight falls inside the frame, against 62 to 81 % in the centre camera's own pose, where the frame's lower edge already cuts the person (`<surface-runs>/ticket-5/side_request_framing.json`).
- Every rendered value is finite. The share of a tile's pixels with alpha ≥ 0.5 is 0.96 to 1.00 in the poses between cameras, 1.00 in the centre pose, and 0.88 to 1.00, 0.69 to 0.96, 0.52 to 1.00 and 0.37 to 0.95 at yaw ±30°, ±45°, ±60° and ±90° (`side_<name>.coverage.json` beside each sheet).
- One model stands out in those numbers. In `stride4`, the tiles at yaw +60° are fully covered at 2.5 s and 4.958 s, and the last is nearly black (mean of the clamped render 0.09, against 0.16 to 0.40 in every other tile of the seven sheets); its tile at yaw −90° and 2.5 s is 95 % covered, against 50 to 64 % in the other six. Something opaque stands between those poses and the person in that model; the other six sheets' numbers show nothing like it.

The sheets are for the owner to look at; this ticket reports no judgement of them.

**On openftgs-synth: the geometry residual and the error of expected depth** (rows 516, 518), from `scripts/gt_depth.py` on the gsplat backend, over every test frame of the scene's test view, with δ = 0.02·S (Q-226 to Q-231). The checkpoints measured, and why:

- **`default-395` of each scene** (steps 35 to 37 of `docs/prd/validation-runs.md`): the default training configuration at spec revision 549o, with ꟻLIP as the perceptual network. It is what a later test's run "with the field off" repeats, and none of the three had a `GT_DEPTH.json`.
- **`default` of each scene** (steps 2, 12 and 16, trained before 549o with LPIPS): openftgs issue 328 left their figures beside the runs on 2026-10-22. Run again here, each file equals the stored one byte for byte, so the script at this commit reproduces that record.
- **The other trainings of the default configuration before 549o:** kitchen's `seed1` (step 5; issue 328's stored file, not run again) and `default-326` (step 20), studio's `default-325` and `default-341` (steps 23 and 27). They give the spread between trainings of one configuration.
- **Not measured:** `tau4e-4`, `reg15k` and their periodic checkpoints, which are other configurations; issue 328's files for them stay beside the runs.

The geometry residual r is a primitive's camera-space depth minus the ground-truth depth at the pixel its centre projects to, over the primitives the render reports visible with σ_eff ≥ 1/255, weighted by σ_eff (Q-230). A share "in front" is the weight with r < −kδ, and "behind" with r > kδ.

- **The residual's shares are steady between trainings, the error of expected depth is not.** Over the three trainings of the default configuration before 549o, the six shares differ by at most 0.003 on kitchen and 0.004 on studio. The median absolute error of expected depth runs from 0.207 to 0.295 m on kitchen and from 0.111 to 0.162 m on studio, up to 47 % above its lowest value, and by more within a region: from 0.112 to 0.203 m on kitchen's dynamic pixels, and from 0.181 to 0.309 m on studio's static pixels.
- **At 549o the shares are lower than before it,** by up to 0.017, which is more than the earlier trainings differ by: in front on kitchen (0.040 against 0.050 at δ) and courtyard (0.015 against 0.018), behind on studio (0.412 against 0.422 to 0.426) and courtyard (0.161 against 0.170). The error of expected depth is inside the earlier runs' range on kitchen and on studio's pixels as a whole, below it on kitchen's dynamic pixels (0.045 m), above it on studio's dynamic pixels (0.084 m), and higher on courtyard (0.897 m against 0.692 m). Each scene has one training at 549o, so the spread there is not known.
- **Most of the weight off the surface is behind it,** not in front. In the 549o runs, at δ, 29 % of the weight is behind on kitchen, 16 % on courtyard and 41 % on studio, against 1 to 4 % in front; on dynamic pixels 44 to 65 % is behind. The residual counts a primitive whether or not something hides it. Expected depth, in which a hidden primitive weighs little, errs toward the camera on every scene (mean error −0.19 to −0.98 m).

**What a later test compares with.**

- **On private scene A:** the row of the first table with its test view, its cap and its init, or a baseline it trains itself. The far-5 cm share compares only against the same init points file, and it says more with its near-side part beside it. A difference under 0.01 in the far-5 cm share, or under 0.004 in the under-two-frames share, is within what a second seed gave. The sheet is rendered from the same request.
- **On openftgs-synth:** the `default-395` rows. On the residual's shares a difference above 0.005 is more than the earlier trainings differed by. On the error of expected depth one run per arm cannot show "no worse": the earlier trainings of one configuration differ by up to 47 %.

**What is open.**

- **The fourth nine-camera model.** The notes' scorer gave it a far-5 cm share of 0.234 and an under-two-frames share of 0.518, in the score file beside the link, against an init points file that file does not name. Measuring it here, and rendering its sheet, needs the owner's word that the link's target may be opened.
- **The sheets** have not been seen by anyone yet.

**The commands.** Each ran in the ticket's worktree with `OMP_NUM_THREADS=4 MKL_NUM_THREADS=4 PYTHONPATH=<worktree>/src:<worktree>/harness/src` and the shared `.venv`'s Python; the CPU runs also had `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=` empty. The scripts under `<scratch>/ticket-5/` are not committed. `$T` is `<scratch>/ticket-5`, `$O` is `<surface-runs>/ticket-5`, `$G` is `$OPENFTGS_DATASETS/synth/ground_truth`.

The GPU commands ran one at a time, from `$T/run_gpu.sh`, between 01:44:29 and 01:53:05; the card's memory peaked at 5,422 MiB during `gt_depth.py` and 7,737 MiB during `ftgs render`, about 1,400 MiB of it in use before they started. The private scene's folders were read and never written.

### 2.7 Probe: agreement of prior maps between cameras

*Ticket #12's section.* One estimator of depth that clears the bar, cited from section 3.3: its depth lifted to 3D with the scene manifest's cameras, against the ground-truth depth on `kitchen`, which gives the estimator's own error, and between the cameras of each camera pair on private scene A's person; how scale and shift are set, the same way on both scenes; what the figures mean for H2's premise.

**What was measured, and with what.** One estimator's depth on the images of `kitchen` and of private scene A, lifted to 3D with each scene manifest's cameras, on 2026-11-15 and 16, by scratch scripts under `<scratch>/ticket-12`, which are not committed: "Runs", at the end of this section, lists every command. The package is the worktree's, whose `src/` and `scripts/` are `main`'s at 93eb58e, on Python 3.14, torch 2.15.0+cu132, NumPy 2.5.4, Pillow 13.0.0 and one RTX 4090, within six CPU threads. The results are in `<surface-runs>/ticket-12`. No image of private scene A was opened, and no prior map or picture made from one was looked at: scripts read the images and wrote numbers (row 620). Nothing derived from them left the machine: the estimator ran from local files, with `HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1`, `TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE=1` and `HF_HUB_DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1` set, and its code makes no network call (row 619). Camera names are the manifests', as in section 2.5. Lengths on private scene A are given in centimetres on the notes' reading of the manifest's unit as a metre, and beside them as a share of the depth, which needs no such reading and is what compares the two scenes: `kitchen`'s surfaces lie about 3.5 m from its cameras and the person about 1.05 units from private scene A's.

**The estimator** is the one section 3.3 names for this probe: Depth Anything V2 Small, weights file `depth_anything_v2_vits.pth` of the repository `depth-anything/Depth-Anything-V2-Small`. Its terms and its verdict are the estimators table's and are not judged again here: the publisher's model card and the code repository's README both put the Small model under Apache-2.0, and the verdict is **Pass** (section 3.3, the first Depth Anything V2 row; rows 519 and 520, checked again in row 607).

- **The weights file** was downloaded once, without credentials, from the publisher's repository at its commit 03876f8, the commit of the estimators table: 99,218,434 bytes, SHA-256 `715fade13be8f229f8a70cc02066f656f2423a59effd0579197bbf57860e1378` (row 618; Q-7: outside the test suite). It sits in `<scratch>/ticket-12/weights` and is loaded with `torch.load(..., weights_only=True)`.
- **The model code** is the publisher's, from its code repository at commit a561b84, read after its license file (Apache-2.0) and copied unchanged into `<scratch>/ticket-12/da2-src`: `depth_anything_v2/dpt.py`, `dinov2.py`, `dinov2_layers/` and `util/` (row 619). No package was installed for it, into the shared `.venv` or anywhere else. The files import OpenCV and torchvision for their image preprocessing only, so the scratch script gives the import two empty stand-ins, calls the model's `forward`, and does the publisher's preprocessing in PyTorch: RGB ÷ 255, a bicubic resize without antialiasing that keeps the aspect ratio and makes the shorter side 518 px with both sides multiples of 14, then ImageNet's mean and standard deviation. A `kitchen` image (1352 × 1014) enters the network at 686 × 518 and one of private scene A (736 × 1280) at 518 × 896. The output is resized to the image with bilinear interpolation and `align_corners=True`, as the publisher's `infer_image` does. PyTorch's bicubic kernel stands in for OpenCV's; the two were not compared.
- **The output** is affine-invariant inverse depth (row 521): a map d per image, zero or more after the model's last ReLU, such that the depth z along the camera's optical axis follows 1/z = s·d + t for a scale s and a shift t that the estimator leaves open, per image.
- **The images.** On `kitchen`, every tenth frame (30 frames) of each of the 20 cameras, 600 images, and frames 1, 101 and 201 of each, 60 more. On private scene A, all 120 frames of the nine cameras, 1,080 images. The network takes 11 ms per `kitchen` image and 15 ms per image of private scene A, at a peak of 279 and 394 MiB of GPU memory.

**The measure.** Everything below is computed per camera pair (A, B) and frame, by one script, `agree.py`, in the same way on both scenes.

- **Pairs.** On private scene A, D-22's 7 pairs, 14.5° or 15.0° apart (section 2.5). On `kitchen`, three sets: D-22's 13 pairs among the 19 training cameras, 2.8° to 6.6° apart; `cam00`–`cam01`, 6.3° apart, added so that the test camera `cam00`, which D-66 keeps out of initialization, is measured too; and section 2.5's six pairs about 15° apart (`cam02`–`cam06`, `cam03`–`cam07`, `cam05`–`cam09`, `cam06`–`cam10`, `cam08`–`cam12`, `cam12`–`cam16`), which are the like-for-like floor for private scene A's pairs.
- **Matches.** The dense matcher's matches between the two images of a pair at one frame, admitted as D-22 admits them (both ends inside their images, certainty at least 0.5). On private scene A they are section 2.5's, kept by ticket #4 for every frame and read from its files (rows 614, 620). On `kitchen` they were made again for the frames above, by ticket #4's script unchanged and, for `cam00`–`cam01`, by a short script that calls the same matcher builder, settings and deterministic context (D-58, Q-106; the weights of row 613, already in the cache, row 622).
- **Anchors.** A uniform sample of 50,000 of a pair-frame's admitted matches is triangulated with the manifest's two cameras by the package's own DLT (`triangulate_dlt`). A triangulated match is an anchor when it is finite, lies in front of both cameras and is seen under at least 1°: D-22's filters without the 2 px reprojection filter. An anchor gives one pixel and one depth in image A, and one pixel and one depth in image B. Why the 2 px filter is left out is in "How scale and shift are set", below.
- **Alignment.** For each of the two images, s and t are fitted to its anchors: the least-squares line of the anchors' inverse depths 1/z against the map's values d at the anchors' pixels, made robust by iterative reweighting (Tukey's biweight at 4.685 times the scale, the scale 1.4826 times the median absolute residual, 30 rounds from a plain least-squares start; row 623).
- **Lift.** A pixel's depth is z = 1/(s·d + t), and its point is the camera's centre plus z times the pixel's ray, with the manifest's K and pose in §1.4's conventions: the pixel (u, v) is sampled at (u + 0.5, v + 0.5), and z is the depth along the optical axis (D-28). A pixel whose s·d + t is not positive has no depth.
- **The guard,** set on the estimated depth so that it is the same on both scenes: a pixel is left out when the lifted depth varies by more than 5% inside the 5 × 5 pixels around it, or has no depth. This is Q-228's rule (a relative jump above 5%), carried from the ground truth to the estimate and widened from 3 × 3 to 5 × 5 because the estimator's map, resized from the network's resolution, spreads a depth step over several pixels. A comparison is left out when either of its two ends is. The guard is computed on the depth of the whole-image alignment (below) under either alignment, so that both leave out the same pixels.
- **The distance between two cameras' surfaces.** Every kept pixel of A is lifted and projected into B. Where it lands, B's lifted depth is read (bilinear, in inverse depth). The two lifted points then lie on one ray of B, and the distance is the gap between them along that ray: |z_B − z_AB| times the ray's length at unit depth, z_AB being the depth of A's point in B. The relative gap is |z_B − z_AB| / z_AB. The same from B into A, and the two directions are pooled.
- **Occlusion.** The gap along a ray also reads a point of A that B cannot see, as the distance to whatever hides it. Such a point always lies behind B's surface. So the figures are given twice: over all kept comparisons, and over those where A's point lies in front of B's surface ("in front"), which no occlusion explains. On `kitchen` the ground truth says which of A's points B sees, and the figures are given over those as well.
- **Regions.** On `kitchen`, by the ground truth's masks of dynamic objects at the pixel (row 611): static and dynamic. On private scene A, by the depth the whole-image alignment (below) lifts a pixel to, as section 2.5 split its matches: the **person** is the pixels of rule a's crop lifted to a depth of 0.5 to 1.8 units, and the **background** the pixels lifted to 2.2 units or more. The pixels the loose rule of section 2.5 calls placeholder pixels are left out of both. A comparison belongs to the region of the pixel it starts from; the figures are also given over the comparisons whose point lands in the same region of the other image ("both ends").
- **Matched and unmatched.** Section 2.5 could speak only for the pixels the matcher admits, 18% of the crop. Each kept pixel is here marked by whether the matcher's grid point at it has an admitted match, and the distance is given for both groups.
- **One camera, two frames.** Where two consecutive frames of a pair are both measured (every frame on private scene A; frames 0 and 1, 100 and 101, 200 and 201 of the six wide pairs on `kitchen`), each camera's two lifted depths, each from its own frame's alignment, are compared at the same pixel. Nothing in it depends on a second camera: it reads how far the estimator and the alignment repeat themselves.
- **Against the ground truth,** on `kitchen`: the lifted depth minus the ground-truth depth (planar z in metres at the pixel centre; rows 611, 621), on the surface pixels away from depth edges by Q-228's rule on the ground truth (`scripts/gt_depth.py`'s `away_from_edges`), split into static and dynamic pixels. The ground-truth frame of an image is found as `scripts/gt_depth.py` finds it (Q-231).
- **Figures.** The median and the 90th percentile over the kept pixels of all frames together, from histograms with 100 bins per decade, which place a quantile within 1.2%; and the median of each pair-frame alone.

**How scale and shift are set, and why.** The alignment is the heart of the measure: a lifted surface is only as good as its s and t. Private scene A has no ground-truth depth, so both must come from something the two scenes share. The choice is the dense matcher's triangulated matches between the two cameras being compared, per pair and frame, the same on both scenes.

- **Why the matches.** They are what initialization has: the init points are these matches triangulated (D-22), so a technique that fits a prior map to the init points, or to a depth rendered from primitives that started on them, sees this alignment. A kept checkpoint's expected depth was the other candidate that both scenes have, and it was set aside: on private scene A it holds the shell, the thing in question, and on `kitchen` it lies 0.2 to 0.3 m in front of the surface at the median (section 2.6). A fit between the two cameras alone, with no anchor, was set aside too: for a matched pixel pair it is the triangulation again, and without matches it leaves the common scale of two nearly parallel views loose.
- **Why per pair.** On private scene A the pairs compared are D-22's own, so the two images of a pair share their anchors. `kitchen` is aligned the same way: the six wide pairs are anchored on their own matches, not on those of D-22's narrower pairs. Otherwise `kitchen`'s two images would share no anchor, its floor would be looser, and private scene A would look better against it than it is.
- **Why without the 2 px filter.** D-22 keeps a triangulated match only if it reprojects within 2 px in both images. On `kitchen` that filter removes nothing (section 2.5), so the rule with it and the rule without it are the same rule there. On private scene A it removes the matches on which the two cameras disagree most, and with them, in some pair-frames, every match on the person ("Private scene A: the anchors", below). An alignment on D-22's points would then be set by the points where the cameras already agree, or by the background alone. The filter is left out of the anchors, and the figures with it are given beside the others.
- **Two alignments.** With the **whole-image** alignment, an image's s and t are fitted to all its anchors: one scale and one shift per image, as the ticket words it. With the **regional** alignment, they are fitted to the anchors of the region compared and used on that region alone: on private scene A the anchors at a depth of 0.5 to 1.8 units for the person and those at 2.2 or more for the background; on `kitchen` the anchors on dynamic pixels for the dynamic objects and the others for the static pixels. Which pixels a region holds and which the guard leaves out stay as defined above, and a comparison counts only with both ends in the region, since a scale and a shift fitted to one region say nothing of the other's depth. The regional alignment was meant as the more favourable to agreement, since it absorbs whatever a scale and a shift fitted to the person alone can. On `kitchen`'s dynamic objects it is. On private scene A's person it is not, so the two are given side by side throughout.
- **What the alignment cannot show.** Three things, which bound every figure on private scene A.
  - The two images of a pair are fitted to the same triangulated points. Whatever offset in depth the two prior maps have against each other, on average over the anchors, is fitted away. The distance that remains is what a scale and a shift per image cannot absorb: a difference of shape, not of position. How far the triangulated points of two pairs agree with each other is measured apart, without any prior map ("One camera, two partners", below; row 624).
  - The anchors carry the manifest's cameras, which are off by tenths of a degree (section 2.5), and the disagreement of the two images along the epipolar line, which the residual of section 2.5 does not read: a shift of 1 px along the line moves a triangulated point at the person's depth by 5 mm in depth (z²/(f·b), with z = 1.05, f = 789 px and a baseline b of 0.27). Both move the two lifted surfaces together and leave little between them ("Checks of the measure").
  - The anchors exist where the matcher is certain. Elsewhere the alignment is carried over from them.

**Checks of the measure.** The whole script was run on prior maps whose answer is known: for each `kitchen` image a map d = a/z + b made from its ground-truth depth z, with a and b different for every camera, on the six wide pairs at frames 0, 100 and 200 (18 pair-frames, 36 images), with the matcher's real matches. Lengths are in millimetres.

| Check | Lifted depth against the ground truth, static: median, 90th percentile (mm) | The same as a share of the depth, median | Between cameras, where B sees A's point, static: median, 90th percentile (mm) | Between cameras, in front, static: median, 90th percentile (mm) | Between cameras, all kept comparisons, static: median, 90th percentile (mm) | Kept comparisons that B does not see; their relative gap, median |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The maps as made, whole-image alignment | 0.12, 0.25 | 0.003% | 0.04, 0.09 | 0.04, 0.09 | 0.05, 0.15 | 7.8%; 27% |
| The same, regional alignment | 0.12, 0.25 | 0.003% | 0.04, 0.09 | 0.04, 0.09 | 0.04, 0.11 | 6.8%; 30% |
| Camera B turned by 0.3° along the epipolar lines | 112, 167 | 2.98% | 9.3, 24.4 | 8.8, 23.4 | 10.4, 30.7 | 9.9%; 21% |
| Camera B turned by 0.1° across them | 2.2, 3.9 | 0.06% | 2.9, 14.0 | 2.9, 14.7 | 3.4, 33.1 | 9.4%; 24% |

- **The conventions hold.** The fit returns each image's scale within 1.4·10⁻⁴ of its true value and its shift within 9·10⁻⁵ per metre, in all 36 images. The lifted depth then lies 0.12 mm from the ground truth at the median, which is the matcher's own error carried through the triangulation, and two cameras' lifted surfaces lie 0.04 mm apart wherever the second camera sees the first's point. The pixel centres, the depth along the optical axis and the lift are therefore read as the manifest and the ground truth mean them.
- **The regional alignment is looser on a small region.** `kitchen`'s dynamic objects hold 3 to 4% of the anchors, within a narrow range of depths, and a scale and a shift fitted to them alone trade against each other: on the dynamic pixels the lifted depth is then 0.5 mm off at the median and 2.2 mm at the 90th percentile, where the whole-image alignment gives 0.09 and 0.18 mm, and the scale is off by up to 1.6%. That is still a hundred times under the estimator's own error below.
- **Occlusion, and what "in front" does.** With perfect maps the guard still keeps points the second camera cannot see: 7.8% of the kept comparisons on static pixels and 8.1% on dynamic ones, at pairs 15° apart, each read as the distance to what hides it, a quarter of the depth. Asking for both ends in the region lowers that to 6.8% and 3.0%. The median does not feel them. The 90th percentile does once they pass a tenth of the comparisons, which they nearly do. Over the comparisons in front, the figures equal those over the points the ground truth says are seen (0.04 and 0.09 mm), so "in front" is used below as the reading that occlusion does not touch, on both scenes. It needs no ground truth.
- **A camera that is slightly off.** Camera B was turned about its own centre before anything else, so that the triangulation, the lift and the projection all use the wrong camera, as they would on private scene A. Turned by 0.3° along the epipolar lines, a direction section 2.5's residual cannot see, it moves both lifted surfaces 11 cm from the ground truth, 3.0% of the depth, and leaves 0.9 cm between them, 0.28% of the depth: the turn adds nearly the same amount to the inverse depth of every anchor, and the two shifts absorb it. Turned by 0.1° across the lines, which is the size section 2.5 fitted on private scene A's pairs (0.10° to 0.41°), it moves the lifted depth by 2.2 mm and puts 2.9 mm between the surfaces, 0.07% of the depth. So an error of the manifest's cameras of the size section 2.5 found shows in this measure as a few tenths of a percent of the depth at most, and an error along the lines hardly shows at all: the measure cannot tell whether the two lifted surfaces are in the right place, only whether they are in the same place.

**On `kitchen`: the lifted depth against the ground truth.** Every tenth frame of the 20 cameras. An image is aligned on the anchors of each pair it belongs to, so a camera in two of D-22's pairs is aligned twice: 840 alignments for D-22's 13 pairs and `cam00`–`cam01`, and 396 for the six wide pairs with their three extra frames. Q-228's rule keeps 97.9% of the surface pixels, and 3.8% of an image's kept pixels are dynamic at the median (7.8% at most). The error is the lifted depth minus the ground-truth depth; "signed" is the median over the images of each image's median error as a share of the depth, positive when the lifted depth is the farther.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

- **The estimator's own error** is about 1% of the depth on static pixels: 4.2 cm at the median and 16 cm at the 90th percentile over the 20 cameras (1.22% and 4.4%), and 3.3 and 12.7 cm on the ten cameras of the wide pairs (0.91% and 3.8%). By camera the median runs from 0.57% (`cam09`) to 2.39% (`cam16`): it is lowest in the middle of the arc and about 2.3% on its last five cameras.
- **The alignment is not what limits it.** Fitted to the ground truth itself, over all the kept pixels of the image, the scale and the shift give the same error as fitted to the pair's anchors (1.24% against 1.22%; 0.93% against 0.91%). So the anchors set the scale and the shift as well as the ground truth can, on a scene whose cameras agree, and what is left is the shape of the estimator's map.
- **The dynamic objects are misplaced against the room.** With the whole image's scale and shift they lie 12 cm from the ground truth at the median, 3.7% of the depth, on the far side (+3.7% signed), and the oracle does no better (3.8%). With a scale and a shift of their own the error is 4.8 cm, 1.5% (6.5% at the 90th percentile). So more than half of the error on the dynamic objects is one offset per image between them and the room, which no alignment of the whole image removes, and the rest, 1.5%, is their shape.
- **The angle of the pair does not matter to this error,** as it should not: the same images aligned on pairs 3° to 7° apart and on pairs 15° apart read alike on the dynamic objects (3.67% and 3.57%; 1.47% and 1.57% with their own alignment). The static figures differ between the two rows because the rows hold different cameras.

**On `kitchen`: between cameras.** The floor of the comparison on private scene A: one rendered scene through exact, synchronized cameras, so whatever distance the two lifted surfaces have here is the estimator's and the measure's own. The same script, the same guard and the same two alignments.

- **The floor at 15°.** Two cameras 15° apart place a static surface 3.1 cm apart at the median, 0.84% of the depth, and the dynamic objects 9.6 cm apart with the whole-image alignment (2.7%) and 4.7 cm with their own (1.33%, and 5.3% at the 90th percentile). By pair the dynamic objects' median runs from 2.0% to 3.2% with the whole-image alignment and from 1.15% to 1.52% with the regional one; a pair-frame's, from 0.2% to 5.7% with the regional one.
- **It is about the estimator's error against the ground truth,** not √2 times it: 0.84% against 0.91% on static pixels, 1.33% against 1.57% on the dynamic objects. Part of each camera's error is therefore common to two cameras 15° apart and drops out between them, and more of it at D-22's angles (0.68% on static pixels and 0.96% on the dynamic objects).
- **"In front" reads what the ground truth reads.** Over the comparisons in front, the figures are those over the points the ground truth says camera B sees: 0.77% and 2.67% against 0.76% and 2.50% on static pixels, 1.27% and 5.04% against 1.29% and 5.02% on the dynamic objects with the regional alignment. Over all kept comparisons the 90th percentile is higher (4.4% on static pixels), because 8.1% of them are points B does not see.
- **Unmatched pixels are the hard ones here too.** On static pixels the matched comparisons read 0.73% and 2.4%, and the unmatched ones, a fifth of the whole, 2.6% and 41%: where the matcher admits nothing on `kitchen` is mostly where one camera does not see what the other does. On the dynamic objects with the regional alignment, 1.13% and 4.0% against 2.2% and 11.2%.
- **One camera, two frames.** On static pixels the same camera's lifted depths at two consecutive frames, each aligned on its own frame's anchors, differ by 0.4 cm at the median and 1.4 cm at the 90th percentile (0.10% and 0.35% of the depth; the six wide pairs at frames 0 and 1, 100 and 101, 200 and 201). Between two cameras the same surface is eight times farther apart. So the estimator repeats itself closely on this scene from frame to frame, and its error is tied to the viewpoint: it moves with the camera.

**Private scene A: the anchors.** D-22's 7 pairs, all 120 frames, 840 pair-frames and 1,680 alignments. A residual in this table is an anchor's lifted depth against its own triangulated depth after the fit, as the median over a pair's images of each image's median; "signed" is positive when the lifted depth is the farther.

- **Every sampled match is an anchor,** since none lies behind a camera or under 1°. D-22's own filters would keep 67.5% of them, from 47% for `cam1`–`cam4` to 94% for `cam0`–`cam1`, which is section 2.5's figure for D-22's draw (67.4%).
- **With D-22's own anchors the person can be left without any.** Run with all of D-22's filters, `cam1`–`cam4` has no anchor on the person in 28 of its 120 frames and fewer than 100 in 45, too few to align the person on; in its median frame 8% of its anchors lie on the person, against 54% without the 2 px filter. That is the pair with the highest residual on the background in section 2.5 (2.24 px). No other pair loses the person (36% to 69% of their anchors stay on it), and "The variants", below, gives their figures with D-22's anchors.
- **Where the anchors are.** 61% lie on the person, at 0.94 to 1.17 units (5th to 95th percentile), and 38% on the background, at 2.6 to 6.0 units. On `kitchen` the dynamic objects hold 3 to 4% of the anchors. So in the whole-image alignment the person weighs more than the background on private scene A, and `kitchen`'s dynamic objects hardly weigh at all against its room: it is the regional alignment that sets the person beside `kitchen`'s dynamic objects like for like.
- **One scale and one shift do not fit both regions.** After the whole-image alignment the person's anchors lie 4.7 cm from their triangulated depth at the median, 4.8% of it, on the far side, and the background's 13.5% of theirs, on the near side. `kitchen` shows the same thing, smaller: 0.8% on the static anchors and 4.4% on the dynamic ones, also on the far side. On both scenes the estimator's map does not hold the subject and its surroundings in the one affine relation to depth that its output is defined by, and on private scene A the misfit is the larger: 4.8% and 13.5%, against 4.4% and 0.8%.
- **Fitted alone, the person's anchors still miss by 1.8%.** With the regional alignment the person's anchors lie 1.8 cm from their triangulated depth at the median (1.17% to 4.24% by pair), and the background's 2.3%. `kitchen`'s read 1.1% (dynamic) and 0.8% (static). That residual is what a technique that fits a prior map to the init points on the person would be left with: about twice `kitchen`'s.

- **The person.** The two cameras' lifted surfaces lie 2.1 cm apart at the median and 13.1 cm at the 90th percentile with the whole-image alignment (1.85% and 9.6% of the depth), and 2.1 and 13.2 cm with the regional one (2.02% and 10.8%). In front, where occlusion plays no part, 1.81% and 9.9%, and 1.84% and 9.3%.
- **By pair.** With the regional alignment the five side-by-side pairs read 0.93% to 2.10% at the median, and the two pairs whose cameras sit one above the other, `cam1`–`cam4` and `cam2`–`cam5`, 4.6% and 4.8%. Those two are also the highest with the whole-image alignment (2.2% and 2.9%, against 1.25% to 2.2%). Section 2.5's residual does not order the pairs this way: `cam2`–`cam5` has one of the lowest residuals there and the largest distance here.
- **Matched and unmatched pixels.** On the 27% of the person's kept pixels that the matcher admits, the surfaces lie 1.5% of the depth apart at the median and 5.3% at the 90th percentile (1.3% and 5.3% with the regional alignment). On the other 73%, which section 2.5 could not see, 2.0% and 15.1% (2.4% and 14.3%). So the bulk of the unmatched part is not far apart either: its median is 2.4 to 2.6 cm. Its tail is: a tenth of it lies more than 19 to 22 cm apart. `kitchen`'s unmatched pixels have a tail too, and a longer one ("On `kitchen`: between cameras"), most of it occlusion.
- **Over the clip.** The median hardly moves between frames 0 to 79 and frames 80 to 119, where the person is closest (1.76% and 2.00%; 1.84% and 2.26% with the regional alignment). The 90th percentile does: 6.4% and 29% with the whole-image alignment, 8.0% and 14.5% with the regional one. A pair-frame's median ranges from 0.65% to 6.5% with the whole-image alignment and from 0.41% to 8.5% with the regional one.
- **The background** is far apart with the whole-image alignment (8.3% at the median), which is the misfit of the bullet above, and 2.5% with its own (8.3% at the 90th percentile).

- **Most large gaps have both ends on the person.** 9.7% of the person's comparisons are more than a tenth of the depth apart, and 32% of those leave the person; on `kitchen`'s dynamic objects 12.0% are, and 68% of those leave. With both ends on the person the 90th percentile is 7.3% (5.4% in frames 0 to 79 and 11.9% in frames 80 to 119), against 7.4% on `kitchen`'s dynamic objects.
- **In front, with both ends on the person,** the 90th percentile is 6.1% (5.3% early, 7.3% late), against 7.25% on `kitchen`. So with the whole-image alignment the part of the tail that neither occlusion nor a point leaving the person explains is no heavier than `kitchen`'s. With the regional alignment it is: 9.3% in front, against 5.04% on `kitchen`'s dynamic objects. The two pairs whose cameras sit one above the other make the difference (17% and 23% in front; the five others read 2.8% to 8.9%).
- **The rest of the late frames' tail lies behind.** In frames 80 to 119 the comparisons with both ends on the person read 11.9% at the 90th percentile over both sides and 7.3% in front: the excess is points of one camera that lie behind the other camera's surface, on the person. `cam6`–`cam8` (52% and 8.0%) and `cam3`–`cam5` (37% and 9.6%) carry most of it. A part of the body that hides another part from one camera would give exactly this, and more of it as the person comes closer; so would a limb that the two cameras place differently, though that would show in front as well, and in front the tail is `kitchen`'s. The probe cannot tell the two apart beyond that.
- **Points that leave the person** are 7% of the kept comparisons (11% in front), with gaps of several times the depth at their 90th percentile: a point of the person that lands, in the other image, on a pixel lifted to the background or outside that camera's crop. Rule a's crop is the tools' default rectangle on seven cameras, and it holds only 47% to 85% of a camera's near pixels (85% on `cam0`, 47% on `cam7`), so part of these are the crop's doing; "The variants" runs rule b.

**Private scene A: two controls inside the scene.** `kitchen` is a rendered room; private scene A is generated footage of a person. Two figures from the scene itself say how the estimator behaves on that footage with no second camera in play.

- **One camera, two frames.** Each camera's lifted depths at two consecutive frames, each aligned on its own frame's anchors, compared at the same pixel, over all 119 frame steps of the 7 pairs. On the background, which does not move, they differ by 0.37% of the depth at the median and 1.6% at the 90th percentile with the regional alignment (0.88% and 4.2% with the whole-image one). `kitchen`'s static pixels read 0.10% and 0.35%. So the estimator and its alignment repeat themselves about four times less closely on this scene's background than on `kitchen`, before any second camera enters. Two things can stand behind that, and the probe does not separate them: generated footage that changes a little from frame to frame where a rendered room does not, and the background's place in the map, since it holds a small part of the range of d and the person the rest, so that an error of the map is a larger share of the background's depth.
- **The person, two frames.** 0.54% and 1.7% with the regional alignment (0.6 and 2.0 cm), 0.63% and 2.1% with the whole-image one. This figure holds the person's own motion as well: the median depth of the person's anchors goes from 1.26 to 0.83 units over the clip in `cam0`, 0.37 cm per frame on average, about 0.35% of the depth. Between two cameras the person's surfaces are nearly four times farther apart than between two frames of one camera (2.02% against 0.54%); on `kitchen`'s static pixels the ratio is eight.
- **The background, between cameras.** With its own alignment the background's two lifted surfaces lie 2.5% of the depth apart at the median and 8.3% at the 90th percentile: three times `kitchen`'s static pixels at the same angle (0.83% and 3.7%), on a part of the scene that does not move and that section 2.5 found to disagree between cameras mostly through the manifest's cameras. Section 2.5's turns are too small to account for that (a few tenths of a percent, "Checks of the measure"). So on this scene the estimator's maps of a static background already agree three times less well between cameras than on `kitchen`, for the two reasons of the first control or for others. An excess of the person over `kitchen`'s floor, of that size or smaller, can therefore not be put down to the cameras' disagreement about the person.

**Private scene A: one camera, two partners.** The measure above compares the two cameras of a pair after both are aligned on the pair's own anchors, so it cannot see whether two pairs agree with each other. Five cameras of private scene A sit in two of D-22's pairs (`cam1`, `cam3`, `cam4`, `cam5`, `cam6`), and seven of `kitchen`'s do, `cam07` in three. For such a camera and a frame, each grid point of the matcher that has an admitted match in both pairs is triangulated twice, once with each partner, by the same DLT and the same anchor rule (a sample of 50,000 points per image), and the two depths of that one pixel are compared, as |z₂/z₁ − 1|. No prior map enters: this reads the images and the manifest's cameras, three at a time, in depth, which is the direction section 2.5's residual does not read. A figure is the median over a camera's frames of each frame's median, or of its 90th percentile; "signed" is positive when the second partner gives the larger depth. Regions are the pixel's: by the first partner's depth and rule a's crop on private scene A, by the ground truth's masks on `kitchen`.

| Scene | Camera | Its two partners | Frames | Grid points admitted in both pairs, per frame | The person, or `kitchen`'s dynamic objects: median, 90th percentile | Signed | Median (cm) | The background, or `kitchen`'s static pixels: median, 90th percentile | Signed | Median (cm) |

| private scene A | `cam1` | `cam0`, `cam4` | 120 | 66,616 | 1.37%, 2.87% | −0.94% | 1.5 | 1.73%, 5.83% | −0.40% | 5.8 |
| private scene A | `cam3` | `cam4`, `cam5` | 120 | 59,176 | 6.45%, 8.47% | −6.45% | 7.9 | 5.30%, 13.81% | −3.78% | 23.0 |

| private scene A | `cam5` | `cam2`, `cam3` | 120 | 68,508 | 1.57%, 4.04% | +0.37% | 1.6 | 4.98%, 8.46% | +4.96% | 25.2 |
| private scene A | `cam6` | `cam7`, `cam8` | 120 | 47,374 | 5.87%, 7.57% | −5.87% | 5.9 | 3.12%, 5.45% | +2.75% | 11.3 |
| private scene A | the five cameras | – | 600 | 61,506 | 4.02%, 6.07% | – | 4.1 | 4.57%, 8.82% | – | 19.2 |

- **The floor.** On `kitchen` a pixel's two depths differ by 0.09% at the median on static pixels and 0.15% on the dynamic objects, 3 to 5 mm at 3.6 m, for cameras 3° to 7° apart, which triangulate less sharply than private scene A's 15° would.
- **On private scene A they differ by 4%.** On the person, 4.0% of the depth at the median, 4.1 cm, and by camera 1.4% to 6.5%, 1.5 to 7.9 cm: 27 times the floor of `kitchen`'s dynamic objects. On the background, 4.6%.
- **It is an offset, not a scatter.** For `cam3`, `cam4` and `cam6` the signed figure equals the unsigned one: at the median frame one partner places the whole person nearer than the other does, by 3.9% to 6.5%. The 90th percentile is not far above the median (6.1% against 4.0%).
- **Most of it is one fixed relation per camera.** A baseline or a focal length that is not the manifest's scales every depth a pair triangulates, and a turn along the epipolar lines adds one amount to every inverse depth. So errors of the cameras relate one pair's inverse depths to the other's by a scale a and a shift b that do not change with the frame: 1/z₂ = a/z₁ + b (row 625). Fitted once per camera over all its frames, with the alignment's robust fit and with the person and the background weighing alike in every frame:

| Scene | Camera | Its two partners | Before: person or dynamic objects; background or static pixels | Scale a | Shift b (per unit of depth) | After: person or dynamic objects, median; a frame's median, least to most | After: background or static pixels, median; a frame's median, least to most |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `kitchen` | the nine combinations | – | 0.13% to 0.19%; 0.07% to 0.12% | 0.9996 to 1.0001 | −0.0001 to +0.0003 | 0.13% to 0.17%; 0.09% to 0.26% | 0.07% to 0.10%; 0.07% to 0.11% |
| private scene A | `cam1` | `cam0`, `cam4` | 1.34%; 1.73% | 1.009 | −0.0021 | 1.60%; 0.57% to 5.26% | 1.67%; 1.11% to 6.08% |
| private scene A | `cam3` | `cam4`, `cam5` | 6.46%; 5.39% | 1.077 | −0.0045 | 1.30%; 0.66% to 2.77% | 6.84%; 3.66% to 21.94% |
| private scene A | `cam4` | `cam1`, `cam3` | 3.88%; 5.24% | 0.971 | −0.0073 | 1.01%; 0.55% to 2.35% | 2.55%; 1.89% to 6.40% |
| private scene A | `cam5` | `cam2`, `cam3` | 1.55%; 4.99% | 1.010 | −0.0098 | 1.64%; 1.02% to 3.16% | 2.00%; 1.47% to 2.83% |
| private scene A | `cam6` | `cam7`, `cam8` | 5.84%; 3.07% | 1.099 | −0.0363 | 1.51%; 0.66% to 3.74% | 3.67%; 0.11% to 6.46% |

- **What the fit does.** On `kitchen` it finds nothing to remove (a within 0.0004 of 1). On private scene A the scale runs from 0.97 to 1.10 and the shift from −0.002 to −0.036 per unit of depth, which at a baseline of 0.27 is a turn of 0.03° to 0.56°. After it the person's two depths differ by 1.0% to 1.6% at the median frame, by camera, where they differed by 1.3% to 6.5%; `cam1` and `cam5`, which had little offset, gain nothing.
- **What that relation is, the probe does not say.** It is what wrong cameras would give, and four of its five shifts are no larger than the turns section 2.5 fitted. But a scale of 8% to 10% between two pairs (`cam3`, `cam6`) would be an error of more than 2 cm in a baseline of 27 cm, and a person drawn a little nearer for one pair than for the other, alike through the clip, would look the same. For `cam3` and `cam6` the relation that suits the person leaves the background at 6.8% and 3.7%, which an error of the cameras would not; yet with D-22's 2 px filter on, `cam6`'s background follows the person's relation to 2.3%. So neither reading is excluded.
- **What remains changes with the frame.** After the fit the person's two depths still differ by 1.0% to 1.6% of the depth, 1.1 to 1.7 cm, 7 to 11 times `kitchen`'s floor, and a frame's median runs from 0.55% to 5.3%. An error of the cameras is the same in every frame, and the part of a turn that a scale and a shift do not capture, which grows toward the image's edges, comes to a few tenths of a percent at most with these shifts. So most of this part is the images': the person drawn at depths that two pairs do not share, by 2 to 3 px along the epipolar line (5 mm of depth per pixel, "How scale and shift are set"). It is the size of section 2.5's residual across the line (0.9 to 5.2 px by pair), in the direction that residual does not read.
- **A prior map inherits it whole.** One camera's map at one frame, aligned once on each of its two pairs' anchors, is lifted to two depths that differ by 4.2% at the person's median depth with the regional alignment and 4.0% with the whole-image one (5.8% for `cam3` and `cam6`, 0.9% for `cam5`), against 0.11% and 0.06% on `kitchen` (`two_pairs.py`, from the scales and shifts of the runs above). The map is the same in both, so all of that difference comes from the anchors.
- **For initialization,** this says that the init points of two pairs that share a camera lie on two surfaces of the person some centimetres apart before training starts. With D-22's own filters, which init points pass, the person's two depths differ by 4.2% at the median, 1.3% to 6.9% and 1.5 to 8.0 cm by camera, over the 484 of the 600 images that keep points on the person. The shell sits 5 to 25 cm from the body (section 2.1).

**Private scene A: the variants.** How much the person's figures hang on three choices, over the 7 pairs together and every frame. The first two rows are the tables above.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
