Orientation and scale enter only through the size on screen (the technique: row 701) | This is the survey's one pruning measure tried on images that disagree: casual captures with transient distractors. The measure is masked by the robust mask, so a primitive that explains only pixels judged to be distractors has no utilization and goes (row 701). On private scene A no mask says which camera is wrong about the person (decision 4); the placeholder pixels are the one set of pixels known to be wrong (section 2.5) | None for the pruning; the robust mask is section 3.6's (row 701) | The RobustNeRF dataset, PSNR (the table of its Figure 11): 28.72 dB with the pruning, 29.27 without it, where transients leak into the render, and 27.64 with opacity reset in its place. So it costs 0.5 dB on the score and gives cleaner renders by the paper's pictures; the paper notes that PSNR and LPIPS show floaters less clearly than a video does. It leaves 4 to 6 times fewer primitives, 2 to 4.5 times fewer on clean Mip-NeRF 360 scenes at kept quality. Limit it states: a threshold set too high removes parts of the scene that few training images see (row 701) | In the paper, at least 2 times less training time, from the smaller count (row 701); with relocation the count stays at N here, and the measure adds its accumulation per iteration, not timed |
| **Second opacity for a geometry path, with a depth consistency loss between views** (StableGS; Wang et al., 2025, arXiv 2503.18458; handed over by family A by its title). Each primitive gets a second opacity: one render, with the first opacity alone, gives the depth that the depth losses act on, and the final image is rendered with the product of the two | None for the second opacity and the consistency loss (row 702). Its optional prior reads DUSt3R's point maps, which **fail** the bar (section 3.3, DUSt3R's row) | Paper only: neither the paper nor its abstract page links a repository (row 706) | **A change to a quantity or to motion**, with a loss that needs new render outputs: a tenth stored tensor, a second render per view with E3's opacity as it is, and the final image rendered with that opacity times the new one. With the second opacity at 1 and no second render, the default leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched. An exchange model could hold the product as its opacity, with no change of format (§2.2; this ticket's reading) (the technique: row 702) | A differentiable depth map per rendered view, for two views per iteration where an iteration renders one image today (D-26), and a warp between them with the manifest's cameras. Expected depth: section 2.4, cells 2a, 2d and 2e. The paper renders depth as RaDe-GS does, which is new kernels (section 3.1's row); whether expected depth would serve is not tested in it (the technique: row 702) | It splits the base opacity in two and leaves the duration alone. Its analysis is the part that bears on the shell: a cluster of faint primitives off the surface settles where its colour errors against the rays that cross it cancel, and then the colour loss has no gradient left to remove it, on its opacity or its colour (row 702). E8 is a force that analysis leaves out: its gradient on σ does not depend on the colour error (E8). The shell persists under it all the same, at `loss.lambda_reg` up to 0.3 (section 2.1). The depth loss needs two cameras at one timestamp, which a scene manifest's frames give. D-14 and E10 would read the first opacity, and E11's copies take both | Its consistency loss assumes cameras that agree geometrically and whose poses are known: a depth warped from one view must equal the depth rendered in the other. It needs co-visible pairs 16° to 60° apart, and the paper names few views as its limit (row 702). On private scene A the cameras disagree about the person by a median of 1.84 px and the manifest's cameras are off by tenths of a degree (section 2.5): the loss would pull both cameras' versions of the person to one depth, which is what H1 asks and where H2 expects blur. The second opacity is the paper's way of keeping what the constrained geometry cannot explain out of the final image's errors; it was shown on translucent objects, not on inconsistent images | None for the self-supervised part. The optional prior needs point maps from an estimator that fails the bar; maps a user supplies would do, which is section 3.4's matter (row 702) | Mip-NeRF 360's two groups of scenes, PSNR (its Table 1): 31.55 and 26.73 dB without the prior, against 31.11 and 26.65 for the pipeline it builds on and 30.51 and 26.70 for RaDe-GS; the prior adds nothing measurable. The ablations are pictures: no table isolates the second opacity or the consistency loss (row 702) | More memory and computation, which the paper states without a figure (row 702). Here two renders with depth for each of two views, where the default renders one image in 30.9 ms an iteration (B-1 at 549o; `docs/prd/validation-runs.md`, step 38) |
| **Anisotropy regularizer on the ratio of scales** (PhysGaussian; Xie et al., CVPR 2024, arXiv 2311.12198; used by AniGS, arXiv 2412.02684, and handed over by families D and E for it). The mean over the primitives of how far the largest scale over the smallest exceeds a bound r | None: no estimator (row 705) | Paper only: no license was found for PhysGaussian's repository or for AniGS's, so documentation only (rows 706, 707) | **A loss on today's primitives:** it reads `log_scales` alone. A field for it defaults to off and leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched (the technique: rows 705, 703) | None: stored tensors only (row 705) | Neither the base opacity nor the duration. It is zero for today's initial spheres (D-5, D-25) and for every primitive until its ratio passes r, then grows with the ratio. It reads the largest scale over the smallest, so it bounds a disk as it bounds a needle: it works against a flattening loss, which sends the smallest scale to 0 (section 3.1), where the effective-rank row tells the two shapes apart. Scales are constant in time (§1.1), so the term is the same at every timestamp; E11 and E17 keep the ratio. Section 2.6 measured no scale (the technique: rows 705, 703) | AniGS uses it in a setting like private scene A's: views of one person generated by a video model, which disagree subtly. There the disagreement is absorbed by a deformation per view of a canonical model, which is section 3.6's matter, and the regularizer serves another end, spikes when the avatar is posed anew (row 703). No evidence that it helps with the disagreement itself | None (row 705) | Pictures only: fewer burr-like artifacts under large deformation in PhysGaussian (its Figure 8), and spikes in a new pose gone in AniGS at weight 0.001 (its Figure 6b). No table isolates it, and the bound r is not in the parts read (rows 705, 703) | One term over 3N stored values; no figure given (row 705) |

**The counts.** 163 results seen, the eight look-ups by name included; 33 abstracts read, of the cap's 60; 12 papers read in full, the cap's 12; 15 rows. The two search seeds account for 2 abstracts, 2 full reads and 4 rows outside the log. PDC-Net+'s abstract was read to place an estimator and is not counted (section 3, "Method"). Several reads were of the part that bears on this family and of nothing else: 4D-Rotor GS, SpotLessSplats, AniGS and PhysGaussian (rows 688, 701, 703, 705).

**The abstract screen.** A title passes when it suggests a technique that acts on the opacity, the lifetime or the shape of primitives so as to remove faint or stray ones; its abstract is then read and counted. An abstract passes when it describes such a technique, during training or as a pruning step, that is new to the survey or differs from a row in columns 3 to 5, and whose aim is not only a smaller model. A technique whose main ingredient is a surface term on normals or depth, a prior map, a dynamic-scene mechanism or robustness to inconsistent images is named with its family and does not count as a pass.

**Where the line with family A runs.** Constraints that read the scales alone are here, since the ticket names anisotropy and rank constraints: the scale penalty, the effective rank, the ratio of scales. Whatever reads a normal, a depth or neighbouring primitives is section 3.1's, and so is flattening as a way to a surface. The effective-rank term crosses the line once: its second part pushes the smallest scale to 0, which is the flattening loss of section 3.1's first row. One conflict follows from putting the two families side by side: the anisotropy regularizer bounds the very ratio a flattening loss drives up.

**The search seeds.**

- **4C4D** (the owner's): the row on its Neural Decaying Function. Its comparison with a constant decay is in the row on the constant opacity decay, the technique it takes from Binocular3DGS. Its initialization from MASt3R goes to section 3.4. Section 3.5 names it for its training from four cameras.
- **3DGS-MCMC's regularizers:** three rows, the opacity penalty without a temporal factor, the scale penalty and the position noise. Its relocation is E11's source and is not screened. The opacity penalty is where the survey meets what the notes tried: E8 is that penalty with a temporal factor, at the same weight (E8, E9; row 658).

**The recency round** is rounds 1 to 3, restricted to 2025-10 onward. Round 1 is the list of the 72 papers that cite FreeTimeGS, which Semantic Scholar's keyless route returned on 2026-11-15 after failing on 2026-11-13 (rows 661, 500). What the period adds to the rows is SharpTimeGS (2026-02), the one paper read that changes the temporal opacity and puts a loss on the duration; 4C4D (2026-04) is of the same period. Five more techniques of the period passed and were not read: UGOD, Polarized Opacity Prior, TIDI-GS, ReorgGS and StereoGS. One citing paper was set aside: the abstract of FreeTimeGS++ (arXiv 2605.03337) says its baseline is a reproduction of FreeTimeGS, so nothing beyond the abstract page was opened, pending the owner's answer on whether such a paper may be read; it is named for section 3.5 (row 663). GraphiXS, whose full text reports work on a FreeTimeGS implementation, was likewise read as an abstract only (row 700). No cell of this section rests on a reproduction of FreeTimeGS: SharpTimeGS's pictures against FreeTimeGS, which do, are not used (row 689).

**Recorded as tried, not screened.** Each of these only moves a value of section 2.1's table.

- **A larger weight on the opacity penalty.** 3DGS-MCMC varies its own between 0.001 and 0.01 (row 658). Here that is `loss.lambda_reg`, tried at 0.1, 0.3 and 1.0.
- **A higher opacity under which a primitive is removed.** SuGaR removes every primitive under 0.5 after its entropy loss, and Binocular3DGS's test of that loss uses the same bound (rows 630, 687). With nothing deleted (D-42) this is `reloc.dead_opacity`, tried at 0.05 and 0.1.
- **Pruning by opacity with a periodic opacity reset, in place of relocation.** 3DGS's own control, which 4D-Rotor GS keeps, Binocular3DGS drops for its decay and SpotLessSplats replaces by its pruning (rows 688, 687, 701). It is the ablation the notes ran, relocation off with `reloc.prune_opacity`, which resets opacities every 3,000 iterations (section 2.3).
- **A smaller budget of primitives.** 3DGS-MCMC's fixed count and the compression methods met in the rounds (rows 658, 666, 667, 704). Here that is `growth.n_max`.

Two existing fields touch the same ground and are not in the notes' table, so they are not tried: `loss.reg_until`, which ends E8 early (D-10), and `reloc.stop`, the iteration after which the primitives that fall under D-14's threshold stay where they are (D-17; section 2.6 counts 7 to 17 % of them at iteration 30,000). Neither is a technique of the survey; they are named so that a regularizer that pushes opacities down is not judged without the second.

**Passed the abstract screen and not read in full**, the cap of full reads being reached; each is known here by its abstract alone, and none has a row.

- **UGOD** (arXiv 2609.39089): a small head predicts a view-dependent uncertainty per primitive, which lowers its opacity before compositing and sets the odds of a soft dropout; a learned relative of the rows on 4C4D's function and on dropout (row 670).
- **Polarized Opacity Prior** (arXiv 2608.22344): a prior that sends each opacity to 0 or to 1 as the way to a compact model; a relative of the entropy row (row 671).
- **TIDI-GS** (arXiv 2601.09291): pruning of floaters by consistency across viewpoints, spatial isolation and a learned importance, with a loss on monocular depth, which is section 3.4's (row 674).
- **ReorgGS** (arXiv 2605.08739): a converged model is resampled from its own primitives at a low opacity and trained on, as an alternative to an opacity reset (row 676).
- **StereoGS** (arXiv 2606.30545): an opacity decay scaled by each primitive's relative opacity-gradient magnitude; its stereo depth is section 3.4's (row 682).
- **SparseGS** (arXiv 2312.00206): a pruning heuristic against floaters beside depth priors, which are section 3.4's (row 684).
- **Clustered Error Correction** (arXiv 2511.16112): rendering errors classed as missing colour or occlusion and corrected by adding or splitting splats, guided by colour consistency between views; handed over by another family (row 681).

**Handed to other families.** Each is named where it was met and left to its section.

- **To section 3.1 (surface-following primitives):** PCAsplat, a local PCA over neighbouring primitive centres that pulls them to a surface and reaches primitives the current view does not render (row 673); ARGS (row 685); SharpTimeGS's flattening term and its consistency between normals and depth on 4D primitives (row 689); the changed growth statistic the effective-rank paper depends on (row 690); and, by their titles in rounds 2 and 3, Spherical-GOF (arXiv 2603.08503), Geometry-Grounded Gaussian Splatting (arXiv 2601.17835), TriaGS (arXiv 2512.06269), TopoSurfel (arXiv 2608.20687) and G²SR (arXiv 2607.14470). TrimGS's trimming by contribution has its row there.
- **To section 3.4 (prior maps):** 4C4D's initialization from MASt3R (row 655); StableGS's prior from DUSt3R's point maps (row 702); StereoGS's stereo depth, SparseGS's depth priors and TIDI-GS's loss on monocular depth (rows 682, 684, 674); RetimeGS's supervision from optical flow (row 668); SharpTimeGS's initialization from flow, masks and COLMAP points (row 689).
- **To section 3.5 (dynamic surfaces):** FreeTimeGS++, by its abstract only (row 663); Dusting the Phantom, whose row is there (row 664); Multi4D and RetimeGS (rows 665, 668); SharpTimeGS's division of the velocity by the lifespan and its second-stage removal and cloning by a score (row 689); 4C4D, for its four cameras.
- **To section 3.6 (cameras that disagree):** SSA-3DGS, a learnable 2D overlay that takes up artifacts fixed to one camera's image plane, the case of the placeholder pixels (row 675); SpotLessSplats' robust masks (row 701); AniGS's deformation per view of a canonical model for generated views of a person that disagree (row 703); 3R-GS's joint optimization of poses (row 699); GraphiXS (row 700).

**Handed to this family**, as names to check by its own reads.

- **From family A:** SuGaR's entropy loss and its removal under 0.5 are in the entropy row and in the tried list, on family A's read and on what 4D-Rotor GS and Binocular3DGS say of it (rows 630, 688, 687). The opacity term of Gaussian Surfels is cited in the entropy row from family A's read, which records its aim and not its formula or its ablation line; it was not read again, the cap being reached (row 629). StableGS was read and has its row. MVGSR's pruning measure is known by its abstract alone, as a measure from multi-view contributions, a relative of TrimGS's row in section 3.1 and of the utilization row here (row 640).
- **From families D and E:** 4D-Rotor GS's entropy loss, 4DGS-1K's pruning, SpotLessSplats' utilization-based pruning and AniGS's anisotropy regularizer were read and have rows; the last is PhysGaussian's, which AniGS cites and does not restate. 3DGS-MCMC's regularizers as 3R-GS and GraphiXS use them could not be checked: neither abstract mentions them, 3R-GS was not read for want of reads, and GraphiXS's full text stays unread under the interim rule on reproductions (rows 699, 700). Clustered Error Correction is in the list above.

**How the family reached its stop rule.** By both conditions. Rounds 7, 8 and 9 added no technique that passed the abstract screen, and the twelfth full read was reached with 33 of the 60 abstracts used. The look-ups of handed-over names between rounds 7 and 8 did add techniques; they are not search rounds. What the cap left unread is the list above.

**What the rows show, for review 1.** Input to section 4, which chooses.

- **What each technique acts on.** The base opacity: the opacity penalty, the constant decay, the entropy loss, 4C4D's function, and StableGS's second opacity, which splits it. The duration or the temporal opacity: SharpTimeGS's lifespan loss and its flat-top profile, and, by selection, 4DGS-1K's pruning and the utilization-based pruning. The scales: the scale penalty, the effective rank and the anisotropy bound. The rendered opacity at random: dropout and opacity noise. The position noise moves only primitives at D-14's threshold.
- **The base opacity does not tell the shell from the surface.** The medians far from and near the init points are 0.12 and 0.18, and the whole model's is 0.16 (section 2.6). Every technique that presses on the base opacity presses on all three alike, as E8 does, and the notes' three weights for E8 went from mixed to collapse (section 2.1). What the rows add to E8 is a different weighting in time: the decay and the penalty without a temporal factor press every primitive at every iteration, where E8's weight on a two-frame primitive averages one twenty-fourth of that.
- **The two shell measures point to different techniques** (section 2.6). The under-two-frames population, near the init points, is what the duration techniques act on: the lifespan loss lengthens it, 4DGS-1K's score and the utilization measure remove it. The primitives far from every init point are longer-lived and more than half background by opacity; no technique read selects them by a stored value. The ones that could reach them select by support across cameras: the decay, by its own argument, the utilization measure, and StableGS's depth consistency.
- **Six rows are losses on today's primitives:** the opacity penalty without a temporal factor, the entropy loss, the lifespan loss, the scale penalty, the effective rank and the anisotropy bound. Two are isolated by an experiment. The entropy loss gains 0.95 dB on monocular synthetic scenes, loses on dense real ones and overfits from 3 views in another paper's test. The effective rank lowers the Chamfer distance on DTU by a fifth beyond a changed growth statistic it is paired with. The lifespan loss, the only one whose gradient reaches the duration, has no experiment of its own.
- **The steadiest evidence with few cameras is the constant decay's:** three papers measure it, on static scenes from 3 views (plus 0.96 to 3.4 dB, and minus 1.2 dB when another paper removes it) and on a dynamic scene from four cameras (plus 1.7 dB), with a flat optimum in its rate (rows 687, 695, 655). It is a step, not a loss, and its published rate assumes a primitive updated at most iterations.
- **Dead primitives.** Whatever pushes opacities down adds to the 7 to 17 % of primitives already under D-14's threshold at iteration 30,000, where relocation has stopped (section 2.6). While relocation runs, E11 gives a target and each moved copy a share of the target's opacity, 1 − (1 − σ)^(1/n), floored at 0.005, which the decay and the entropy loss then push down again.
- **Cameras that disagree.** No technique here was tested on cameras that disagree about the subject. The utilization measure was tested with distractors, behind a mask this scene does not have; AniGS has the setting and uses its regularizer for another end. The decay's argument and StableGS's loss assume agreement, and under H2 would trade the shell for blur. The paper on dropout offers a measure that needs no ground truth, the variance of renders from random halves of the primitives, which could be taken on the checkpoints of section 2.6.

### 3.3 Family C, part 1: the estimators and the bar

*Ticket #6's section.* Family C's estimators table: for each of the eight kinds of prior map, the estimators a technique could use, each judged against the bar before anything else of it is read. This section screens estimators, not techniques, so it has no screening table; family C's is in section 3.4.

**How the bar is applied.** The bar is decision 3's: the weights' own publisher states a permissive license that allows commercial use, checked per weights file. A verdict rests on what that publisher states about the file: a model card or a license file in the weights' repository, or a README or release page of the publisher. Each row gives the page's address, the commit it was read at and where on the page the statement stands, so that it can be re-opened and checked. Every page was read on 2026-11-14 or 2026-11-15, the date in its row, by anonymous requests; "not gated" is the repository's own public record, and a gated repository is recorded as gated and its gate left alone. A publisher can change its terms, so a verdict holds for the commit and the date in its row and is checked again before anything is adopted. The publisher's repository is the one the estimator's code repository or paper links to; a mirror or a third party's conversion is another publisher's file and has no row unless it is named as such. One row holds one weights file, or several files that one statement covers in the same words; the verdict is then each file's. Where a publisher lists further sizes or formats under the same statement and their cards were not opened, the row says so. No weights were downloaded and no source code was opened: the "Code license" column is the code repository's license file, read before its README.

- **Pass.** The publisher's statement covers the model or its weights (a license in the card of the weights' repository, a license file there, or a README sentence that names the models), and the license is permissive and allows commercial use: Apache-2.0, MIT or BSD, and CC BY 4.0, which the precedent counted with the caveat that its attribution be accepted as permissive (`docs/research/perceptual-alternatives.md`, section 3.1).
- **Fail.** The statement is a non-commercial, research-only or share-alike license; or a license of the publisher's own that conditions commercial use or carries use restrictions that pass on to recipients, which the precedent placed as failing as stated and left to the owner (its section 3.3, DINOv3); or no weights are published.
- **Unclear.** The pages read cannot place the file on either side. The common case is a grant on the code repository only, a license file that names software, with no statement on the weights: the precedent called it borderline and left it to the owner (its sections 3.1 and 3.3). Another is a license in a card that says someone other than the publisher wrote it. A row says which case it is; none is resolved by guessing.

Three rules for mixed cases. Where two statements of one publisher disagree, both are recorded and the stricter decides. Where the publisher's pages say a file is fine-tuned from another model, or runs on one, that base model's own publisher's statement is recorded and the stricter of the two decides, as the precedent failed LPIPS on its trunk and DreamSim on its head (its sections 3.1 and 3.4). An initialization the pages name without a source, and the training data, are recorded and not judged.

The "Supplied maps" column answers yes on every row: each estimator here writes a map per image, and a technique that reads such maps from files does not care what made them. The cell says what a supplied map has to match (relative or metric, a scale and shift per image or per video), and, for a file that fails, that supplied maps are the only route to it: a user with the right to run that estimator runs it outside the package.

**Estimators table.**

**What passes, per kind.** Of the table's 68 rows, 18 pass, 39 fail and 11 are unclear.

| Kind | Estimators | Rows | Pass | Fail | Unclear | What passes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Monocular depth | 6 | 16 | 5 | 6 | 5 | Depth Anything V2 Small, its transformers version and its two metric fine-tunes; Intel's transformers versions of DPT; Marigold V2's seven depth checkpoints |
| Video depth | 6 | 10 | 3 | 6 | 1 | Video Depth Anything Small and its metric version; FlashDepth-S |
| Surface normals | 5, with Marigold's files | 7 | 1 | 4 | 2 | Marigold V2's normals checkpoint |
| Depth and normals made for people | 6 | 6 | 1 | 5 | 0 | DAViD's depth, normals and multi-task models |
| Feed-forward point maps | 6 | 10 | 3 | 7 | 0 | MapAnything's Apache variant; MoGe, MoGe-2 and MoGe-3 (seven files); Depth Anything 3's Small, Base, Mono-Large and Metric-Large |
| 4D geometry with scene flow | 6 | 6 | 0 | 6 | 0 | None |
| Optical flow and point tracks | 6 | 6 | 2 | 2 | 2 | TAPIR, BootsTAPIR, TAPNext and TAPNext++; AllTracker |
| Masks (segmentation and matting) | 6, with DAViD's files | 7 | 3 | 3 | 1 | SAM; SAM 2; DAViD's soft foreground models |

Some passing files serve a kind other than their row's: Depth Anything 3's Mono-Large and Metric-Large and MoGe's models give monocular depth, MoGe-2's `-normal` files and MoGe-3 give normals, and DAViD's depth and normals are for people only.

**Why files fail or stay unclear.** Four patterns account for the 39 failures. The large models of a family are non-commercial where its small ones are permissive (Depth Anything V2, Video Depth Anything, Depth Anything 3, MapAnything's default variant, VGGT's original checkpoint), and so are the fine-tunes of them, whatever their own card says (the metric Base and Large models of Depth Anything V2, FlashDepth-L). Estimators fine-tuned from a diffusion model inherit its terms: OpenRAIL++-M from Stable Diffusion 2 (Marigold v1), the Stability AI Community License from Stable Video Diffusion (ChronoDepth, DepthCrafter, NormalCrafter, MotionCrafter, VideoMaMa). The line that starts at DUSt3R is non-commercial and share-alike from end to end (DUSt3R, MASt3R, MonST3R, St4RTrack). And Meta's newer releases come under agreements of its own, with use restrictions or for research only, two of them behind a gate (Sapiens2, VGGT's commercial checkpoint, SAM 3, CoWTracker). Twice the code is permissive and the weights are not (ViGeo, V-DPM), and five times the statements of one publisher disagree (Depth Anything V2's metric models, Marigold v1-0, Depth Pro, Depth Any Video, Depth Anything 3's Large-1.1): each is recorded with both statements and judged on the stricter.

The eleven unclear rows are the owner's call, as the precedent's borderline rows were. Seven are a permissive license on the code repository that does not name the weights: the original files of Depth Anything V1, MiDaS and ZoeDepth; Metric3D v2; RAFT; WAFT; and BackgroundMattingV2, whose README licenses "the work". Two are a permissive license in a card of the publisher's own Hugging Face repository that says the Hugging Face team wrote it in the publisher's place: the transformers versions of Depth Anything V1 and of ZoeDepth. Intel's transformers versions of DPT pass because their cards say Intel co-wrote them. The other two are GeoWizard, whose CC BY 4.0 statement is on the project and whose base is Stable Diffusion, and FlashDepth's full model, which may hold weights derived from a non-commercial file. Seven rows fail "as stated", under a license of the publisher's own that allows use with conditions which pass on to recipients or depend on revenue (OpenRAIL++-M for Marigold v1-1's two files, the Sapiens2, VGGT and SAM Licenses, the DINOv3 License behind F2S, the Stability AI Community License for VideoMaMa); the precedent left that case to the owner too. CC BY 4.0 would pass with the precedent's caveat, and no pass rests on it.

**For the probe on the agreement of prior maps (section 2.7).** The cleanest pass is Depth Anything V2 Small, `depth_anything_v2_vits.pth` in `depth-anything/Depth-Anything-V2-Small`, or its transformers version. Its publisher names the model under Apache-2.0 in two places that agree, the card of the weights' repository and the code repository's README; the repository is not gated; its backbone is DINOv2's, whose publisher states Apache-2.0 for the weights (row 367); and no base model with other terms stands behind it. Its output is affine-invariant inverse depth, so the probe fits a scale and a shift per image (row 521). Three alternatives pass on grounds as clear. Video Depth Anything Small keeps the same encoder and shares one scale and one shift across a camera's whole video (row 541). Depth Anything 3's Mono-Large predicts depth, not disparity, and its any-view Small and Base models take the scene's known cameras as input (row 585). DAViD's depth model is made for people and trained on synthetic people only (row 569). The paper of Depth Anything V2 says its synthetic training sets hold no people (row 521), which bears on a scene whose subject is one.

**MotionCrafter**, the owner's search seed (row 491), has its row under 4D geometry with scene flow, and fails: its publisher licenses the weights for academic use only, says they are not intended for use within the European Union, and names Stable Video Diffusion as its base model (rows 587, 588). None of the six estimators screened in that kind passes. The kind filled with the six its first two rounds named, so no round looked for a permissive one, and two of the six, MonST3R and MoRe, name no scene-flow output in the pages read. A technique that initializes position and velocity from a point map and scene flow (section 3.4) would have to read them from files a user supplies, or take the two from passing estimators of other kinds: a point map from MoGe or Depth Anything 3, and motion from the point tracks of TAPIR or AllTracker lifted with depth.

**How each kind reached its stop rule.** Seven kinds stopped at six estimators, and surface normals stopped after two consecutive rounds that added none, with five estimators (section 3.3's log). Three choices are stated here so that they can be undone. Marigold V2 is another publisher's model in Marigold's line: it is kept as rows of Marigold, as the newest monocular-depth estimator that passes, and counted apart it would make that kind's seventh. Marigold's and Marigold V2's normals files and DAViD's soft foreground files are rows of estimators counted under another kind. Metric3D v2 and GeoWizard, which give depth and normals from one file, and MoGe, which gives a point map from one image, were named by the monocular-depth rounds and are counted under surface normals and point maps. Every name in a query that no result confirmed is marked in the log (row 555).

**For the other sections.** Column 1 of a screening table cites a row here by estimator and weights file. An estimator a technique names that this table lacks is screened in its family's own table; the rounds met these and did not screen them: MetricAnything, AsyncMDE and UniDepth (monocular depth); NVDS and StableDPT (video depth); StableNormal (normals); HumanNorm and PIFuHD's normal network (people); LingBot-Map, π3 and VGGT-X (point maps); Dynamic Point Maps, Stereo4D and CUT3R (4D geometry); LocoTrack, Jeff-Tracker, Track4World, M2P, MEMFOF, DPFlow and NeuFlow (flow and tracks); Track Anything, Matting Anything 2 and μMatting (masks). ECON was read and has no row: it reconstructs people from the normals of Sapiens (row 573). §2.3's table holds none of the estimators here, and its notes name only BackgroundMattingV2, as an external tool that is not a dependency; so adding one, even one that passes, is the owner's decision. A technique that reads prior maps from files puts no estimator in the package, and for the files that fail it is the only route.

### 3.4 Family C, part 2: techniques that use prior maps

*Ticket #9's section.* The survey of family C's techniques: the losses that use prior maps, and positions, velocities and orientations initialized from them, with the cap of 60 abstracts and 12 full reads.

**Screening table.**

| Technique | 1. Weights' license | 2. Code license | 3. Integration class | 4. Render backends | 5. Fit with the primitives | 6. Cameras that disagree | 7. Prior maps | 8. Evidence | 9. Training cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Depth loss against a monocular depth map aligned per image by one scale and one shift** (DN-Splatter, Turkulainen et al., WACV 2025, arXiv 2403.17822, in an edge-aware logarithmic form; the plain L1 form is the baseline that In Depth We Trust, Xiao et al., 2026, arXiv 2604.05715, measures) | DN-Splatter's experiments use ZoeDepth and Depth Anything (V1), each **unclear** in section 3.3 (ZoeDepth's torch hub models and Intel's transformers versions; `pytorch_model.bin` of `LiheYoung/depth_anything_vit{s,b,l}14` and the `-hf` versions), so neither is treated as passing; one comparison adds Metric3D, whose v2 files are unclear there, the version used not being stated (row T9.12). In Depth We Trust uses Depth Anything V2, size not stated (the Small file passes, Base and Large fail), MoGe-2 (pass), UniDepth V2 (**fail**, this section's table) and Marigold (v1 fails; the release is not stated) (row T9.18). A passing file of the same kind: Depth Anything V2 Small, `depth_anything_v2_vits.pth`, which section 2.7 ran, its two metric fine-tunes, Video Depth Anything Small, Depth Anything 3's Mono-Large and Metric-Large, or MoGe (section 3.3). The swap leaves the loss as it is; what changes is the parameterization the scale and shift are fitted in (inverse depth for Depth Anything V2 Small, as section 2.7 fitted it) and the estimator's own error | DN-Splatter: Apache-2.0, readable; the technique is taken from the paper and the code was not opened (rows T9.12, T9.13). In Depth We Trust: paper only, it links no repository (row T9.18) | **A loss that needs new render outputs:** the mean over pixels of log(1 + the absolute difference of rendered and prior depth), weighted by exp(−∇I) so that it is weaker at image edges, at weight 0.2 (DN-Splatter); or the plain L1 between the two (In Depth We Trust's baseline) (rows T9.12, T9.18). At weight 0, the default, E9's value, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are. The weight is a new `LossConfig` field and the depth a new `RenderOutput` field, both §2.1 signatures (section 2.2); a scratch prototype reaches the depth through Q-226's device without either | Expected depth, the composited z-depth divided by the accumulated alpha, which both papers render (rows T9.12, T9.18): section 2.4, cells 1a and 2a as a channel of the colour pass, the route DN-Splatter takes in one kernel pass, or cells 1d and 2d through the device with no API change; cells 1e and 2e for the reference backend. No median, no distortion | It reads no orientation and no scale, so the constant ones are untouched (§1.1). Its gradient reaches `means`, `velocities` and `times` through z (E1) and `opacity_logits` and `log_durations` through the weights (E3, E4; section 2.4's two parts). An expected depth is a weighted mean along the ray: SparseGS says a model can meet a depth map by changing the transmittance of misplaced primitives instead of moving them, and DNGaussian that a depth loss on all parameters lets primitives escape by lowering opacity or changing shape (rows T9.28, T9.14). Under E3 the opacity the renderer sees is σ·σt(t), so the base opacity and the duration both offer that escape: the loss does not by itself forbid faint layers off the surface (this ticket's reading). A primitive that lives one to four frames (section 2.6) is supervised by the depth maps of those frames only. Relocation and split are untouched; the loss enters E10's gradient statistic only when depth is a channel of the colour pass (section 2.4, "What the device does not give a prototype") | **Through the model's geometry, after an alignment.** Each image's map is compared with the render of that image alone, in absolute depth, so everything turns on what the scale and the shift were fitted to. Both papers fit them to the sparse depth of structure-from-motion points projected into the view (rows T9.12, T9.18); here that is the init points (D-22) or a depth rendered from primitives. Section 2.7 measured that fit on private scene A: one scale and one shift per image leave the person's anchors 4.8% of the depth off and the background's 13.5%, with opposite signs; fitted to the person alone, 1.8%; and one map aligned on the anchors of its camera's two pairs lands at depths 4% apart. So the loss assumes that the per-image maps, once aligned, describe one surface, and on this scene they do not within some centimetres. Evidence that the plain form is fragile even where cameras agree: on Mip-NeRF 360 with 16 to 39 views it gives 21.80 dB against 21.79 without depth on 3DGS and lowers PSNR on 2DGS (21.42 against 21.56); with the weakest estimator on ScanNet++ it costs 0.52 dB; and fewer points for the alignment make it worse than no depth loss (row T9.18). No experiment in either paper has cameras that disagree | One depth map per training image, used at the training image's resolution, and a scale and a shift per image fitted once against sparse depth, as both papers do (rows T9.12, T9.18): the init points seen in that image, or a rendered depth. Depth Anything V2 Small took 15.1 ms per image on private scene A's 1,080 images (section 2.7's runs). Maps the user supplies would do: the loss needs a map per image and, for a relative one, its parameterization. `ftgs-scene` v1 has no key for a map per image (§2.2), so a path per training image is a change to §2.2, the owner's | DN-Splatter, indoor captures of static scenes. With sensor depth, one MuSHRoom scene (Table 4): the depth loss alone takes the mesh F-score from 56.3 to 86.6 and PSNR from 26.6 to 27.1. With monocular depth, six scenes (Table 7): F-score 0.696 against 0.584 and PSNR 24.48 against 24.44; one ScanNet++ scene (Table 8): F-score 0.146 with scale-aligned ZoeDepth against 0.134 with no supervision and 0.583 with sensor depth, and the paper says monocular depth supervision needs further research (row T9.12). In Depth We Trust isolates the plain L1 form as its baseline, with the figures of column 6 (row T9.18) | DN-Splatter: 13.1 against 12.9 minutes for 30,000 iterations with depth composited in the colour kernel (row T9.12). Here, about 2% of an iteration as a channel of the colour pass and about half an iteration more through the device (section 2.4, "The cost") |
| **Hard and soft depth regularization on patch-normalized depth** (DNGaussian, Li et al., CVPR 2024, arXiv 2403.06912; the search seed) | DPT, `dpt_hybrid_384` and `dpt_large_384` (row T9.14): the MiDaS repository's release files are **unclear** in section 3.3, and Intel's transformers versions of the same two networks, `Intel/dpt-hybrid-midas` and `Intel/dpt-large`, **pass**. The paper's Table 10 gives the two networks the same result within 0.1 dB. Any passing monocular depth file would serve as well, since the normalization removes scale and shift patch by patch; whether an inverse-depth output is converted first is not stated in the parts read (row T9.14) | Paper only: the repository is under the Gaussian-Splatting License of Inria and the Max Planck Institut für Informatik, non-commercial (row T9.15) | **A loss that needs new render outputs**, with gradients routed per term: a hard depth, rendered with every opacity replaced by 0.95, whose loss moves the centres only; a soft depth, the ordinary composite, whose loss tunes the opacities only; scales and rotations kept out of both. Each is an L2 with an error tolerance between rendered and monocular depth after a patch-wise normalization, by the patch's mean and standard deviation (local) and by the patch's mean and the image's standard deviation (global), the local term at 0.1 (row T9.14). The routing is a set of stop-gradients, not a change to a quantity. At weight 0 E9, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are; the fields are new `LossConfig` fields | The soft depth is a composite of a scalar per primitive, its distance to the camera centre: section 2.4, cell 1a or 8a, or 1d. The hard depth uses other weights than the colours', so it is not a channel of the colour pass: a second call of `rasterize_to_pixels` with the opacities replaced, or one more pass of the device on a copy whose opacity logits are replaced (cell 1d, 14.8 ms a pass). The reference backend forms its weights in PyTorch and would form them a second time. Neither needs a median or a distortion (row T9.14; section 2.4) | The term is aimed at what the shell is made of: the paper's argument is that under a hard depth a primitive at a wrong position cannot answer by lowering its opacity or changing its shape, so its centre moves (row T9.14); with every opacity at 0.95 a faint primitive in front of the body counts in full. The routing maps onto the stored tensors: hard depth to `means` and, through E1, `velocities` and `times`; soft depth to `opacity_logits`. A static paper cannot say on which side `log_durations` and `times` fall, which enter the opacity through σt(t) (E3, E4), nor whether the hard depth replaces σ alone or the product σ·σt(t): both are choices a spec revision would have to make (this ticket's reading). Lower opacities on misplaced primitives feed relocation's dead test, which reads the base opacity (D-14); E11 and E17 are untouched. No orientation is read | **Within one image only.** Rendered and prior depth are each normalized inside the patch, so no scale, no shift and no anchor is fitted, and the disagreement between pairs' anchors (section 2.7) does not reach the loss. The global term divides by the whole image's standard deviation, which mixes a person and a background that section 2.7 found not to share one affine relation. The paper assumes known poses and three input views, and its own limitation is the one that matters here: against the same renderer without depth it gains 2.7 dB with 3 views, 1.1 dB with 6, and nothing with 9 (23.17 against 23.21 dB), which it puts down to errors in the depth maps once the colours constrain enough, naming the uncertainty of monocular depth as the next step (row T9.14). Private scene A has nine cameras. In Depth We Trust measures the method below a baseline without depth once views are many: 24.25 against 25.59 dB with 48 to 117 views (row T9.18) | One relative depth map per training image; no alignment and no sparse points (row T9.14). Supplied maps would do. `ftgs-scene` v1 carries none (§2.2) | LLFF with 3 views, 6,000 iterations (Table 4, PSNR): a plain L2 depth loss on all parameters 18.14; the same with both normalizations 18.68; hard and soft with the global normalization 18.55; in full 19.12. Without freezing the shapes 17.96, without freezing the centres in the soft term 18.87 (Table 5). Its neural colour renderer is on in every row (row T9.14) | Two more depth renders an iteration: through the device, about one iteration more (section 2.4). The paper gives no time per term (row T9.14) |
| **Patch-based Pearson correlation depth loss** (SparseGS, Xiong et al., 3DV 2025, arXiv 2312.00206; also the depth term of MOSAIC-GS, arXiv 2601.05368, and a baseline in DN-Splatter and In Depth We Trust) | SparseGS: Marigold, whose v1 files **fail** in section 3.3; the paper says any pretrained depth estimator would do (row T9.28). MOSAIC-GS: a sensor's depth or Depth Anything (V1), **unclear** (row T9.23). DN-Splatter's comparison: ZoeDepth or Metric3D, both **unclear** (row T9.12). A passing file: Depth Anything V2 Small or any other passing depth file of section 3.3. The swap's one cost: the correlation is invariant to a scale and a shift in the map's own parameterization, and Depth Anything V2 Small's is inverse depth, which is not affine in depth; either the rendered depth is inverted before the correlation or the mismatch is accepted inside a small patch (this ticket's reading; no paper read says which) | Paper only: SparseGS's repository is under the Gaussian-Splatting License, non-commercial (row T9.34) | **A loss that needs new render outputs:** one minus the Pearson correlation between patches of the rendered depth and of the prior, over randomly drawn non-overlapping patches of 32 or 64 pixels, with a global term over the image; both at 0.15 (row T9.28). At weight 0 E9, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are; new `LossConfig` and `RenderOutput` fields as in the first row | On alpha-blended depth: expected depth, section 2.4, cells 1a and 2a, or 1d and 2d. SparseGS's own form correlates a softmax-scaled depth, a mean of depths with weights w·exp(5w) in each pixel's blending weights w, and prunes with a mode depth, the depth of the primitive with the largest weight (row T9.28). Neither is a composite with E6's weights, so, like the median of cell 3a, the gsplat backend could form them only from the pair list, which section 2.4 did not measure, and the reference backend from its own weights (cell 3e). The loss works without them at a measured cost: 18.45 dB on alpha-blended depth against 18.86 on softmax depth, from 17.49 (row T9.28) | The tensors reached are the first row's. A point cloud per image, fused per image, keeps one surface per camera. MonoFusion reports the same of independently lifted monocular depth, as duplicated parts (row T9.21). Evidence with few cameras: 3 to 9 views of static real scenes. Its stated limit: where the multi-view depth is badly wrong the optimization cannot correct the misaligned start and ghosting remains (row T9.27) | Per image used for initialization: a depth consistent between views with a confidence, and a monocular depth map, fused offline (row T9.27). Supplied as an init points file, which puts no estimator in the package (§2.2) | Mip-NeRF 360 with 3 views (Table 2, PSNR): 15.74 with the fused depth against 15.61 with the multi-view depth alone, whose floaters its Figure 8 shows. The dense start is not compared with structure-from-motion points (row T9.27) | None at training time beyond the cost of more primitives; the fusion is preprocessing, a sparse linear solve per image, not timed in the parts read (row T9.27) |
| **Monocular depth aligned between cameras and over time to a multi-view point map, as the start and as a depth target** (MonoFusion, Wang et al., ICCV 2025, arXiv 2507.23782; a search seed of family D, read here for its depth alignment) | MoGe for the monocular depth, which **passes**; DUSt3R for the multi-view target, which **fails**; SAM 2 for the background masks, which **passes** (section 3.3; row T9.21). In DUSt3R's place, as in the row above: MapAnything's Apache variant or Depth Anything 3's any-view models with the known cameras, or D-22's triangulated points on the background. The paper says a multi-view predictor does worse on people than a monocular one, since people are outliers in its training data (row T9.21), and that would hold for a substitute | Paper only: the repository has no license file and its README states no license (row T9.22) | Two parts. **None of the five classes: an initialization:** DUSt3R on the cameras' images at one time, its global optimization held to the known intrinsics and extrinsics, gives metric depth maps consistent between views; each monocular map gets a scale and a shift per camera and frame by least squares against those maps on background pixels only, the target averaged over time since the cameras are static; the aligned maps are unprojected to points, five primitives per pixel, with scales from the pixel's footprint. **A loss that needs new render outputs:** an L1 between the rendered depth and the aligned map (weight 1.0), beside L1 losses on a rendered mask and on rendered features (row T9.21). The points can be supplied as an `ftgs-init` v1 file with no spec change (§1.3); the scale rule cannot (D-25). At weight 0 and with `roma_init`, E9, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are | For the loss, expected depth (row T9.21): section 2.4, cells 1a and 2a, or 1d and 2d. For the start, nothing | Its setting is the nearest to private scene A among these rows: people, a few static cameras with known poses, four of them about 90° apart. Two of its findings bear on today's steps. Scales from nearest neighbours left very large primitives that filled empty space, and it took the pixel's footprint instead (D-25's rule is the 3-NN distance). And velocities from 2D tracks lifted with per-frame depth did much worse with four cameras than in the monocular case, because the person's estimated depth flickers between frames (its Appendix D; row T9.21); E15 takes its velocity from positions at two init frames as well (D-23). Its motion bases and rigidity loss are family D's (section 3.5) | **It compares the cameras through one multi-view target, and fits each map on the background only.** That is the alignment section 2.7 measured the limit of: one scale and one shift do not hold the person and the background together, on private scene A (4.8% of the depth off on the person and 13.5% on the background, with opposite signs, when fitted to both) or on `kitchen` (3.7% on the dynamic objects against 1% on the room); fitted on the background alone the whole misfit would fall on the person, which section 2.7 did not measure. The paper says as much: the foreground may remain noisy after the alignment, and no alignment in the output space repairs a person's depth that is wrong seen from other views (row T9.21). Evidence with few cameras: its whole evaluation, four real synchronized cameras; none with cameras that disagree | Per camera and frame: a monocular depth map and a background mask; per time or once, a multi-view metric depth (row T9.21). MoGe takes 60 ms an image with ViT-L (section 3.3). The masks need a prompt per moving object, which the paper lists as a limit. Supplied as an init points file and a depth map per training image; `ftgs-scene` v1 carries no depth map (§2.2) | Its own four-camera scenes (Table 3, PSNR): 29.55 with the aligned depth against 26.19 with independently lifted depth, and mask IoU 0.73 against 0.60. Panoptic Studio, views 45° from the training cameras (Table 2): 25.73 against 21.56 for Shape of Motion run on the four views and 23.31 for Dynamic 3D Gaussians (row T9.21) | About 30 minutes on an A6000 at 512 × 288 for the whole method; the alignment is a least-squares fit per camera and frame (row T9.21). The depth render costs as in the first row |
| **Primitives added where moving content has no points, from depth and scene flow** (4D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild, Kim et al., NeurIPS 2024, arXiv 2411.08879) | Depth and scene flow both from the method of Zhang et al., "Consistent depth of moving objects in video", which is trained per video and is **unclear** (this section's table) (rows T9.24, T9.32). Passing substitutes: depth from Depth Anything V2 Small or Video Depth Anything Small; the moving pixels from thresholded flow (SEA-RAFT's passing file), from tracks (TAPIR's family, AllTracker) or from SAM 2's masks (section 3.3; this section's table). The swap's cost: a per-video optimization gave the paper one consistent depth per video, and per-image depth needs the alignment of the rows above | Paper only: it names the code bases it builds on and links no repository of its own (row T9.24) | **None of the five classes: an initialization.** Moving pixels are found from scene flow, a subset is drawn, and each gives a new primitive at the pixel back-projected with its depth, coloured from the image (row T9.24). The positions can be supplied as an `ftgs-init` v1 file with no spec change (§1.3). With `roma_init` as the source, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are | Nothing: an initialization reaches no render backend (row T9.24) | The gap it fills in its paper is not today's: structure from motion leaves moving regions empty, while §1.3's steps 1 to 3 triangulate matches at every init frame, moving or not. Today's gap is the pixels the matcher does not admit: 82% of the person's crop (section 2.5) and 73% of the person's kept pixels (section 2.7). It sets no velocity: the paper's motion is a deformation field, and it says its densification ignores temporal consistency (row T9.24); supplied points without velocities start at v = 0, the paper's "w/o 4D initialization" ablation (§1.3) | **It lifts per-camera depth with the cameras,** as the two rows above, and inherits the alignment in the same way (section 2.7). Its paper is monocular. Its uncertainty-aware terms weight a smoothness prior by how little the training pixels support a primitive; they use no prior map and are named for sections 3.1 and 3.6 (row T9.24) | A depth map per image used for initialization and a mask of moving pixels, from flow, tracks or segmentation (row T9.24). Supplied as an init points file (§2.2) | One DyCheck scene (Table 3, mPSNR): 17.04 with the added primitives against 15.74 without, both with the depth and flow losses, and 15.25 for the baseline (row T9.24) | None at training time; preprocessing only (row T9.24) |
| **Motion initialized from point tracks lifted with depth** (MOSAIC-GS, Morkva et al., CVPR 2026, arXiv 2601.05368) | RAFT for the flow that finds moving regions, **unclear**; SAM 2 for the masks, **pass**; BootsTAPIR for the tracks, **pass**, and CoTracker on one dataset, which **fails** there on CoTracker3's files; depth from a sensor or from Depth Anything (V1), **unclear** (section 3.3; row T9.23). A passing set exists for every part: TAPIR's family or AllTracker for tracks, Depth Anything V2 Small or Video Depth Anything Small for depth, SAM 2 for masks, SEA-RAFT's passing file for flow (section 3.3; this section's table) | Paper only: it links no repository (row T9.23) | **None of the five classes: an initialization.** 10,000 query points in the moving regions are tracked through the video, lifted to 3D with the depth maps and the cameras, refined per object by a rigid fit between consecutive frames, and the result is solved into each dynamic primitive's trajectory before the photometric optimization (row T9.23). The paper's trajectory is a Poly-Fourier curve, which would be a change to motion; under E1 the same lifted tracks give a velocity, the difference of a track's positions at two frames over the time between them, and `velocities` is a key of `ftgs-init` v1, so points with such velocities can be supplied with no spec change (§1.3). The rigid refinement per object is family D's (section 3.5). With `roma_init` as the source, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are | Nothing: an initialization reaches no render backend (row T9.23) | It replaces what E15 guesses: E15 pairs each point with its nearest neighbour in the next init frame, with no outlier filter (D-23), and a track gives the correspondence itself. E1 and a duration of one init interval (D-21) ask only for a track's two nearest frames, so short tracks suffice, where the paper tracks through the whole video (row T9.23). The velocity along the ray is a difference of two frames' depths, which is where the method is weakest (column 6) | **Within one camera over time,** and through that camera's depth at each frame. The tracks never compare cameras. The component of the velocity along the ray is the difference of two depths, each with its own alignment: on private scene A one camera's lifted depth of the person at two consecutive frames, each aligned on its own frame's anchors, differs by 0.54% of the depth, 0.6 cm, at the median and 2.0 cm at the 90th percentile (section 2.7), while the person approaches by about 0.5 over the 120 frames of the clip (section 2.6), some 0.4 cm a frame with the manifest's unit read as a metre: the flicker is the size of the motion. MonoFusion met this with four cameras (the row above). A video depth estimator that keeps one scale and one shift for a camera's whole video, Video Depth Anything Small (section 3.3), removes the part that comes from aligning each frame apart. The paper's own limits: it inherits the errors of its masks and tracks, and a person can be split into several instances (row T9.23) | Per camera: tracks of chosen points through its video with visibility, a depth map per frame, masks of the moving regions (row T9.23). Supplied as an init points file with velocities, which puts no estimator in the package (§2.2) | DyCheck, monocular video from a moving camera (Table 5, mPSNR): 18.40 in full against 16.99 without initializing the trajectories, and 18.11 without the rigid refinement (row T9.23) | None at training time; the tracking and lifting are preprocessing, and the paper's optimization takes about 5 minutes (row T9.23) |
| **Position and velocity from a point map and scene flow** (the outputs of MotionCrafter, Zhu et al., 2026, arXiv 2602.08961; the owner's search seed. No paper read uses them this way) | MotionCrafter **fails** in section 3.3: its publisher licenses the weights for academic use only, and it is a fine-tune of Stable Video Diffusion; none of the six estimators of 4D geometry with scene flow screened there passes (rows 587, 588). Two routes remain. Prior maps the user supplies: a user with the right to run MotionCrafter writes the init points outside the package. Or a passing point-map estimator with passing point tracks, as section 3.3 lists them: MoGe or Depth Anything 3 for the point map, TAPIR's family or AllTracker lifted with depth for the motion, which is the row above. What the second route costs: MotionCrafter gives a flow for every pixel of every frame in one frame of reference per video, with no post-optimization (row T9.25); the substitute gives tracks of chosen points or a dense field from a query frame, and takes the motion along the ray from differences of per-frame depth, with the flicker of the row above. | Documentation only: the repository's license restricts use to academic purposes (row 588). The method is taken from its paper (row T9.25) | **None of the five classes: an initialization.** The estimator returns, per frame of one video, a point for every pixel and the flow that carries it to the next frame (row T9.25). A primitive stores exactly these (§1.1): μx from the point map at an init frame, v from the flow over the frame interval, in place of steps 2 to 5 (matching, triangulation, E15). Both are keys of `ftgs-init` v1, so points made outside the package can be supplied with no spec change (§1.3). Run inside the package it would be a new source in `openftgs.init` and an estimator added to §2.3, which the bar excludes for this one. With `roma_init` as the source, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are as they are | Nothing: an initialization reaches no render backend, and the estimator runs before training (row T9.25) | The fit with E1 is exact in form, and the estimator's frame of reference is the obstacle. Its points and flow are in the coordinate system of the video's first frame, and it predicts no camera; its training normalizes each scene's scale, and its evaluation aligns a sequence by one scale and one shift (row T9.25). A multi-camera scene is nine separate videos to it: each camera's output has to be brought into the manifest's frame, by that camera's known pose and a scale and a shift per video fitted to something triangulated. Its README's example runs 25 frames at 320 × 640 (row 588); how longer clips are joined is not in the parts read. E15's nearest-neighbour velocity and D-22's filters would no longer apply; the budget of step 7 still does | **Per camera, then through the manifest's cameras.** Each video is predicted alone, so the cameras meet only in the alignment, and section 2.7's finding holds for any map aligned to triangulated points on private scene A: it lands at the anchors' depth, and the anchors of two pairs that share a camera differ by 4% of the depth, most of it one fixed relation per camera. Nine per-camera point clouds are nine surfaces of the person until training reconciles them, which is the setting H2 describes. One scale and one shift for a whole video, as its evaluation fits them, would hold the frames of a camera together better than a fit per frame (the row above). No evidence of any kind: the paper measures its point maps and flow against ground truth and starts no reconstruction from them (row T9.25) | Per camera: a point map and a scene-flow map per frame, at the estimator's resolution, sampled down to the init budget. Supplied as an init points file with positions and velocities; for this estimator that is the only route (decision 3). The weights are a 669 MB VAE and two UNets of 3.05 GB (row 587); no time per frame is stated in the pages read | **None that isolates it.** The paper's figures are for the maps themselves: 38.64% and 25.0% better geometry and motion than earlier methods, by its abstract (row T9.25). The nearest measured cases are the two rows above, on monocular video: points placed from depth where scene flow marks motion, 17.04 against 15.74 mPSNR (row T9.24), and trajectories from lifted tracks, 18.40 against 16.99 (row T9.23); and MonoFusion's finding that such velocities did badly with four cameras (row T9.21). FreeTimeGS++'s abstract names an initialization guided by UFM; its full text is unread under the interim rule on reproductions (row T9.4) | None at training time; running the estimator is preprocessing, on a diffusion model of several gigabytes (row 587) |

**The counts.** 212 results seen: 120 in the eleven web rounds and the look-up of one seed, the 72 titles of round 12, and 20 in two searches by name. 48 abstracts read, of the cap's 60; 12 papers read in full, the cap's 12; 15 rows. The license files of seven of those papers' repositories were looked for, and the terms of four estimators (rows T9.13, T9.15, T9.17, T9.20, T9.22, T9.33, T9.34; rows T9.29 to T9.32).

**The abstract screen,** as applied from round 1. A title passes when it suggests a loss or an initialization that reads a map predicted by a pretrained network; its abstract is then read and counted. An abstract passes when it describes such a technique for a per-scene optimization, not a feed-forward network or an editing pipeline, and when the technique is new to the survey or differs from one already passed in the form of its loss, in the prior map it reads or in what it initializes. A technique whose main ingredient is another family's is named with that family and does not count as a pass.

**The search seeds.** Each has a row.

- **DN-Splatter:** three rows, the aligned depth loss, the normal loss and the dense initialization with orientations. Its loss on the smallest scale is family A's flattening loss (section 3.1).
- **DNGaussian:** the row on hard and soft depth regularization.
- **Initialization from the DUSt3R and VGGT line:** the rows of RI3D and MonoFusion, both of which start from DUSt3R's point maps, with the passing estimators that could stand in. Starts from VGGT passed the abstract screen three times (Gesplat, Fast 3D Foundation Model Initialized Gaussian Splatting, Ground4D) and none was read in full; 4C4D starts from MASt3R (row 655). DUSt3R, MASt3R and VGGT all fail the bar (section 3.3).
- **MotionCrafter:** the last row. The estimator fails the bar, and no paper read initializes a reconstruction from its outputs, so the row is this ticket's reading of the estimator's paper against §1.3, with the two nearest measured techniques above it.

**The recency round** is rounds 3 and 12: a web search that names FreeTimeGS, and the titles of the 72 papers that cite it, 69 of them dated 2025-10 or later (row 661). Five of the twelve papers read in full are of 2025-10 or later: GC-4DGS (2025-11), MOSAIC-GS (2026-01), MotionCrafter (2026-02), MoRGS (2026-03) and In Depth We Trust (2026-04). Of the papers that cite FreeTimeGS, MotionCrafter has its row; UniFusion and Sparse Dynamic Cameras are known by their abstracts; RetimeGS and SharpTimeGS by section 3.2's reads (rows 668, 689); and FreeTimeGS++ by its abstract only, its full text unread under the interim rule on papers that reproduce FreeTimeGS (rows T9.4, 663). No cell of this section rests on an authors' reproduction of FreeTimeGS, and none of the twelve papers read describes one.

**How the family reached its stop rule.** By the cap of full reads: the twelfth was reached with 48 of the 60 abstracts used. The other condition was not met. Rounds 10 and 12 added no technique that passed the abstract screen, but they were not consecutive, and round 11 added three: the search had not run dry, and a larger cap would add rows. The owner can move the caps.

**Passed the abstract screen and not read in full**, the cap being reached; each is known here by its abstract alone, and none has a row.

- **UniFusion** (arXiv 2609.05888): monocular depth maps across views and time represented as spatio-temporal neural fields and aligned jointly, without separating foreground from background and without masks or tracks, with a multi-view depth-order loss; the aligned depths initialize and supervise a 4D reconstruction from few cameras. By its abstract it is aimed at what section 2.7 found, that one scale and one shift per image do not hold the person and the background together; it cites FreeTimeGS (rows T9.35, 661).
- **D²-4DGS** (arXiv 2608.01588): for few cameras, monocular depth aligned with multi-view geometric depth and checked against it, the verified anchors driving pruning, depth supervision and densification; the class of GC-4DGS's two rows (row T9.4).
- **4D Reconstruction from Sparse Dynamic Cameras** (arXiv 2606.04593): 3D tracks initialized by joining feature matches between cameras with point tracks within a camera, and a depth-ordering loss it calls robust to noise; relatives of the rows on lifted tracks and on ranking (row T9.35).
- **Sparse-View 4D Gaussian Splatting via Spatiotemporal Priors and Generative Assistance** (arXiv 2610.04606): a challenge entry for six cameras; masks guide the initialization and the densification, monocular depth aligned to metric scale regularizes the background only, and optical flow constrains the projected motion (row T9.35).
- **MonoInstance** (arXiv 2503.18363): the depths of each segmented instance from several views are aligned in one 3D space, the density of the resulting points gives an uncertainty for the depth prior, and a mask term takes over where it is high (row T9.11).
- **Shape of Motion** (arXiv 2407.13764): monocular depth maps and long 2D tracks consolidated into one dynamic scene; MOSAIC-GS and MonoFusion describe and build on its lifted tracks (rows T9.6, T9.21, T9.23).
- **GaussianFlow** (arXiv 2403.12365) and **Motion-aware 3DGS** (arXiv 2403.11447): the flow loss of MoRGS's row in two earlier forms, the second with an uncertainty on the flow (row T9.7).
- **MVPGS** (arXiv 2409.14316) and **FatesGS** (arXiv 2501.04628): ranking losses on monocular depth for few views of static scenes, the first beside learned multi-view stereo (row T9.8).
- **FSGS** (arXiv 2312.00451), **HDGS** (arXiv 2505.22279), **LoopSparseGS** (arXiv 2408.00254) and **Depth Aware 3DGS** (arXiv 2410.11080): monocular depth losses for few views, by correlation at one or several scales, by alignment to sparse depth in windows, and by a scale-invariant form (rows T9.2, T9.4).
- **Gesplat** (arXiv 2510.10097), **Fast 3D Foundation Model Initialized Gaussian Splatting** (arXiv 2607.03209), **Ground4D** (arXiv 2606.28828) and **Pi-GS** (arXiv 2602.03327): starts from the point maps of VGGT or π³, the last with an uncertainty-guided depth loss (rows T9.5, T9.10, T9.11).
- **GaussianRoom** (arXiv 2405.19671) and **Endo-4DGS** (arXiv 2401.16416): a normal-prior term for textureless rooms, and a confidence-guided depth loss for endoscopic video (rows T9.3, T9.10).

**Handed to other families.** Each is named where it was met and left to its section.

- **To section 3.1 (surface-following primitives):** the loss on the smallest scale that DN-Splatter and VCR-GauS both add; VCR-GauS's densification of large surface primitives and its split along the largest axis; GaussianRoom's signed distance field that guides densification; G3Splat and TriaGS, by abstract and title; StableGS's self-supervised depth consistency; and the uncertainty-weighted smoothing of rendered depth in 4D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild, which reads no prior map (rows T9.12, T9.19, T9.3, T9.5, T9.11, T9.24).
- **To section 3.2 (opacity and floaters):** StableGS's two opacities, whose row is there; SparseGS's pruning and its mode depth as a measure of floaters; and a paper on false transparency, arXiv 2510.15736, by its title (rows T9.5, T9.28).
- **To section 3.5 (dynamic surfaces):** MonoFusion's motion bases and rigidity loss; MOSAIC-GS's rigid refinement per object, its Poly-Fourier trajectories and its split into static and dynamic primitives; MoRGS's offset field and motion confidence; Shape of Motion's motion bases; Splatography, 4DSurf, AT-GS, the compact representation of arXiv 2311.12897 and the motion bases of arXiv 2603.25058; FreeTimeGS++, by its abstract only; and, by their titles in round 12, arXiv 2603.00952, 2602.03538, 2603.24994, 2604.00538, 2605.23672 and 2611.04666 (rows T9.21, T9.23, T9.26, T9.6, T9.4, T9.7, T9.36).
- **To section 3.6 (cameras that disagree):** SparseCam4D, which models generated observations that are inconsistent with a distortion field over space and time; the diffusion models of RI3D, of the challenge entry and of 4D Gaussian Splatting in the Wild; the joint refinement of poses in Gesplat and in Fast 3D Foundation Model Initialized Gaussian Splatting; USplat4D, by its title; and, by their titles in round 12, two papers on multi-view video diffusion for people, arXiv 2606.13655 and 2510.07190. GC-4DGS's consistency check and VCR-GauS's confidence weight are that family's idea of weighting by trust, applied to a prior map; their rows stay here (rows T9.4, T9.27, T9.35, T9.24, T9.5, T9.36).
- **Body templates, recorded and not searched for** (decision 1): the silhouette losses of OccGaussian and SplatArmor, which round 10's results describe, and the keypoint fitting of arXiv 2607.09125 (row T9.36). A loss between rendered alpha and a foreground mask was met only inside such methods and has no row.

**Handed to this family**, as names to check by its own reads.

- **From family A:** the normal-prior loss of Gaussian Surfels, the monocular normals of SolidGS and GausSurf, GVGS's alignment of monocular depth, SparseSurf's stereo depth, Sparse2DGS's start from learned multi-view stereo, 2D-SuGaR, GSPlane, DRGSplat and arXiv 2607.03765. None was read, the cap being reached. Their classes have rows: the normal loss, the aligned depth loss and the stereo depth.
- **From family B:** SparseGS was read and has its row. 4C4D's start from MASt3R is in section 3.2's read, which records no ablation of it (row 655). StableGS's prior from DUSt3R's point maps is in section 3.2's read too: on Mip-NeRF 360 with all views it changes PSNR by 0.01 dB (31.54 and 26.74 with it, 31.55 and 26.73 without) (row 702). SharpTimeGS, a 4D method that cites FreeTimeGS, initializes from RAFT flow, SAM 2 masks and COLMAP points (row 689), and RetimeGS's abstract names an initialization and a supervision from optical flow (row 668): both are the nearest relatives of the rows on flow and on lifted tracks, and neither was read here. StereoGS and TIDI-GS were not read (rows 682, 674).
- **From families D and E:** MonoFusion and GC-4DGS were read and have rows. UniFusion, D²-4DGS, Sparse Dynamic Cameras and the challenge entry are in the list above, by their abstracts. AniGS's normal loss is cited in the normal loss's row from section 3.2's read (row 703). SPIn-NeRF's abstract names no depth term, and the paper was not read (row T9.35). An initial velocity from optical flow, as FreeTimeGS++ is said to use it, is named only: the paper's full text is unread under the interim rule, and its estimator was not screened here.

**Estimators met and not screened.** Kosmo-GFM (arXiv 2611.16840, of 2026-11-12) predicts, by its abstract, depth, cameras, correspondences, dynamic 3D points and scene flow, which would make it an estimator of the one kind where section 3.3 found none that passes; its project page returned no text, and no weights page or license statement of it was found (row T9.36). π³, which Pi-GS starts from, and UFM, which FreeTimeGS++'s abstract names, were not looked up. Each would be screened in section 3.3's way before any row leans on it.

**What the rows show, for review 1.** Input to section 4, which chooses.

- **Every loss here can be rendered today.** Each row's loss reads expected depth, composited normals, normals taken from the depth map, or a composited 2D vector, all of which section 2.4 supplies as channels of the colour pass or through the device. None needs the median or the pairwise distortion, so none raises the pair-list question. Two forms fall outside: SparseGS's softmax and mode depths, which need each pixel's individual weights, and VCR-GauS's intersection depth, which needs new kernels or the 2DGS ones; both papers measure what is lost without them.
- **Column 6 sorts the losses into four groups, and section 2.7 says what each meets on private scene A.** Losses taken within one image (the Pearson loss, the ranking loss, DNGaussian's normalized depth, the gradient term, the normal loss) fit no scale, shift or anchor, so the 4% by which the pairs' anchors disagree does not reach them; by the same invariance they shape a surface and cannot place it, so none of them can by itself bring a layer of faint primitives back onto the body. Losses that pass through an alignment (the aligned depth loss, MonoFusion's depth target, the absolute term of In Depth We Trust) do place the surface, at the depth of whatever they were aligned to, and inherit the misfit of one scale and shift per image and the pairs' disagreement. One technique compares the cameras and drops what disagrees (GC-4DGS's check), which on this scene would leave it silent on the person where it is needed. Two use the model's own renders to decide where a prior acts or how much (the mask of In Depth We Trust, VCR-GauS's confidence weight).
- **The measured gains come from few views and shrink as views are added.** DNGaussian gains 2.7 dB with 3 views and nothing with 9; In Depth We Trust gains 0.47 dB with 16 to 39 views and 0.13 dB with 48 to 117, and measures the plain aligned loss at no gain or a loss; DN-Splatter's monocular depth helps a mesh and leaves PSNR where it was. Those papers add a depth prior where the colours under-constrain the geometry. On private scene A nine cameras constrain it and contradict one another, a case no paper read tests. So for depth priors H1 rests on thinner evidence from papers than the row count suggests, and nothing read speaks against H2.
- **Three terms are aimed at the shell's mechanism,** by this ticket's reading of their papers: primitives that answer a constraint by turning faint instead of moving. They are DNGaussian's hard depth, which counts every primitive as nearly opaque; the mask of In Depth We Trust, which finds the pixels whose rendered depth changes with the viewpoint; and SparseGS's softmax depth, which the gsplat backend cannot supply. The first two are losses that need new render outputs and nothing else.
- **Initialization from files needs no spec change for positions and velocities.** `ftgs-init` v1 already carries both, and supplied points skip steps 1 to 6 (§1.3), so a producer outside the package can be tried against today's code, which no loss here can. What a start from prior maps adds is coverage of the 73% of the person's kept pixels that have no admitted match; what it inherits is the alignment, as section 2.7 says of any map aligned to triangulated points. Orientation and an anisotropic scale at the start need a new key in §2.2 and a change to steps 8 and 9.
- **The owner's seed cannot be adopted as it is, and its substitute has a known weak point.** MotionCrafter fails the bar and predicts per camera, in each video's own frame and scale. The passing substitute, point tracks lifted with depth, takes the motion along the ray from differences of per-frame depth, and on private scene A that flicker is the size of the person's motion; a video depth estimator with one scale and shift per video, or a flow loss on the velocities, which needs no depth difference, are the two ways round it that the rows name.
- **For every kind of map a row needs, a passing estimator exists, except scene flow.** The papers' own choices mostly fail or are unclear (Omnidata, DSINE, DUSt3R, Marigold v1, ZoeDepth, Depth Anything V1, RAFT, MVSFormer); section 3.3's passing files stand in at no cost to the form of any loss here. Every technique also works from maps the user supplies, and each then needs what `ftgs-scene` v1 lacks: a path to a prior map per training image (§2.2).

## 4. The finalists and review 1

*Ticket #13's section.* One screening table and one estimators table, consolidated from sections 3.1 to 3.6; H2's premise, answered with the probes' figures (sections 2.5 to 2.7); two to four finalists, at least one of them only a loss on today's primitives, each with the reason it was chosen; the proposed criteria for training tests, starting from the PRD's proposal; per finalist, the route for trying it and its GPU hours; the owner's answers at review 1, with their date.

## 5. The test plan

*Ticket #14's section.* For each finalist the owner kept at review 1, before anything trains: the spec items it would touch, by identifier, and how; the fields of the training configuration it would add, with their defaults, and whether the default leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched; the prior maps it needs and how they would reach training; the runs, with the criteria agreed at review 1. A sketched signature or field is a proposal for the owner's spec revision, not a Q-n candidate.

## 6. The results

*Ticket #15's section.* For every finalist review 1 cleared to train, its results against the agreed criteria; or the record that none was cleared, or that a finalist waits for its spec revision and is tested later behind its field.

## 7. The recommendation

*Ticket #15's section.* Which techniques to adopt, each as a field of the training configuration that defaults to off, or that none should be adopted.

## 8. What adopting it would change

*Ticket #15's section.* For each recommended technique, over the PRD's list: §1.1 and E5 to E6 if a quantity or the primitive's shape changes, and E1 to E4 if orientation varies with time; E7 and E9 for new loss terms; §1.3 Growth, E10, E11 and E17 for constrained primitives; §1.3 Initialization and E15 for initialization from prior maps; §2.1's `RenderOutput`, `render` and `LossConfig`; §2.2's `ftgs-scene` and `ftgs-if`; §2.3 for any estimator, weights or dependency; U-7, U-8 and U-12; §3.3's adapter contract; §2.4 for cost.

## 9. The sources

*Ticket #15's section.* The clean-room record in full, completing the opening's: the source-log rows of every ticket of the PRD, what was read only as documentation, what was not opened, and any incident.
